1 もともと AfD は、欧州債務危機の際にメルケル政権による南欧諸国への救済措置に不満を抱く経済学者らを中心に結成され、
「教授政党」とも呼ばれていた。当初は EU やユーロ圏からの離脱など、主に経済・通貨政策を掲げており、必ずしも現在のよ
うな排外主義的な政党ではなかったと言われている。しかし、その後は党内で右派的勢力が強くなったことに加え、シリアなど
から多くの難民が流入した 2015 年の欧州難民危機と、当時のメルケル政権による移民・難民政策への世論の反発を背景に排外
主義的な主張を前面に押し出す政党へと変質していった 
2 メクレンブルク＝フォアポンメルン州、ブランデンブルク州、ザクセン＝アンハルト州、ザクセン州、テューリンゲン州 
3 現在、AfD が州議会第一党となっているテューリンゲン州、ザクセン＝アンハルト州、メクレンブルク＝フォアポンメルン州
はいずれも新連邦州だ 
4 田中（2019） 
5 Arzheimer（2017）は、旧東ドイツ地域では戦後から東西統一前までドイツ社会主義統一党（SED）が支配的地位を占め、政党
間競争や自由選挙を経験してこなかったため、西ドイツのような特定政党への長期的な帰属意識が形成されにくかったと指摘し
ている。そのため、統一後に CDU や SPD などの西ドイツの政党が活動を拡大しても強固で安定した支持基盤を築くことは容易
ではなく、AfD のような新興政党が支持を獲得しやすい条件の一つになったとみられる 
6 猛暑が欧州の経済・物価に与える影響については川畑・北川（2026）を参照 
7 また、全国的にも、連邦憲法擁護庁は 2025 年に AfD 全体を「確定的な極右」と位置付けた（現在は司法判断が出るまで保留
されている） 
8 ドイツでは、州政府の構成員が連邦参議院（上院）において各州を代表する仕組みとなっている。各州には人口に応じて 3～6
票が配分され、法案等の審議・議決を通じて州の利害を連邦政治に反映する。今回の州議会選挙後に AfD が州政府に参画すれ
ば、連邦参議院における審議や投票を通じて連邦政治にも直接的に影響を及ぼすことになる。なお、各州の票は分割せず一括し
て投じなければならず、州政府を構成する政党間で投票方針を統一できない場合、当該州は事実上棄権となる 
9 「防火壁」を維持することで、AfD に「主要政党が民意を軽視している」という批判材料を与え、かえって同党を勢いづかせ
ているとも言われている

22 億ユーロ（同州の税収の約 4 分の 1 に相当）の財源が不足すると試算している 
11 ザクセン＝アンハルト州を分析対象とした Gropp and Reifschneider（2026）は、AfD 主導の州政権成立に伴って排外的な政治
風土が強まり、移民の流入が減少すれば、2027～31 年の 5 年間で約 1 万 4,000 人の追加的な人手不足が生じ、18～32 億ユーロ
（同州 GDP の約 2～4％）の付加価値が失われると試算している。なお、Dorsch et al.（2026）についても言えることだが、州支
部と連邦レベルではスタンスの相違点も多いため単純化はできない点には留意が必要だ 
12 自国の主権強化や治安維持等の目的での防衛費増額には賛成している

みずほ経済 EXPRESS

対日直接投資拡大で国内供給力の強化へ 
～戦略 17 分野でみた外資誘致の現状と課題～

調査部  エコノミスト 阿部大樹 
080-3709-6077 taiki.abe@mizuho-rt.co.jp 
■ 高まる国内投資の機運。 国内供給力強化に向けては対内直接投資の取り込みも重要に 
政府は2026年7月、 日本成長戦略会議において、 国内供給力の強化に向けた官民投資ロードマップを
決定した。とりわけ経済安全保障上の重要性や成長期待が高い17の戦略分野においては、2040年度ま
でに累計370兆円超の官民投資が想定されている（図表1）。日本政策投資銀行「2026年度設備投資計
画調査」では、大企業の約58％、中堅企業の約43％が戦略分野における国内投資を強化すると答えて
おり、多くの企業が政府の方針を前向きに受け止めていることがうかがえる。 
国内投資促進の主軸を担うのは政府や国内民間企業だが、国内供給力を強化していくうえでは、外
国資本の取り込み、すなわち対内直接投資の拡大を促すことも重要だ。対内直接投資とは、外国企業
が日本企業への出資や融資を通じて、日本国内の企業経営・事業に継続的に関わることを目的とした
投資のことをいう。政府も対日直接投資推進会議で、戦略分野ごとに有望な外国企業を発掘し誘致す
る方針を掲げている。 
対内直接投資は、 投資形態の違いから四つに分類できる （図表2） 。 このうち、 「合併・買収」 や 「企

### Page 2

2

業・財務再編」は国内供給力の強化につながらない可能性もあるが、「グリーンフィールド投資」と
「能力増強投資」は工場・生産ラインなど生産資本の増加をともない、供給網のレジリエンス向上や
国内の供給力強化につながりやすい形態だ。本稿では、これら二つの形態を合わせて「国内供給力強
化投資」と呼ぶこととする。以下では「国内供給力強化投資」に焦点を絞り、戦略分野ごとに直近5年
間の投資流入状況を確認したうえで、対内直接投資の今後の増加余地と誘致のあり方を検討する。 
■ AI・データセンター関連投資等のグローバルな機運が国内供給力強化投資をけん引  
日本の対内直接投資は、2010年代半ば以降、拡大傾向にある。国際収支統計で集計される対内直接
投資の動向を示した図表3をみると、 その力強い伸びが顕著に見て取れる。GDP比でも1.0％ 程 度（2025
年）まで増加しており、外国企業の国内投資志向が高まりつつある証左と言えよう。 
先行きは、さらなる対内直接投資の拡大が期待できそうだ。予定段階の個別投資案件を集計してい
るfDi Marketsのデータで国内供給力強化投資の動向を確認すると、2020年代に入って投資金額が大幅
に拡大していることがわかる （図表4）。同データでみられる投資拡大の動きは、クロスボーダー金融
取引の実績を計上している国際収支統計でも今後顕在化してくるとみられる。 
さらに、個別投資案件のデータを確認することで、国際収支統計では把握できなかった対内直接投
資の性質・内容を詳細に分析することが可能である。戦略分野別に国内供給力強化投資をみると、AI・
半導体分野や、データセンター関連が含まれるデジタル・サイバーセキュリティ分野で投資金額が大
きく、 かつ大幅な伸びが確認できる （図表5） 。 世界的なAIブームを受けたデータセンター関連投資の
活発化が背景とみられる。半導体製造設備やデータセンター関連の投資には巨額の資本が必要になる
ため、投資金額を件数で割った一件あたりの投資額が大規模化しやすく、大型投資案件が投資額全体
を押し上げていることがうかがえる。 
次に投資金額が大きかったのは資源・エネルギー安全保障・GX分野だ。具体的には、発電所などの
電力供給設備への投資案件が押し上げている。太陽光発電・洋上風力発電などの再生可能エネルギー
市場で海外企業の参入が増えた結果とみられ、脱炭素化を志向するグローバルな機運が追い風になっ
図表3 対内直接投資 図表4 国内供給力強化投資

3

ていると言える。加えて、電力需要の大きいデータセンターの立地を最適化するため、電力源と通信
インフラを一体的に整備する「ワットビット連携」が推進される中で、電力供給設備への投資需要が
高まっていることも当該分野の投資押上げに寄与しているとみられる。 
これらの分野では引き続きグローバルトレンドに沿った投資流入増が見込めそうである一方、造船
分野や防衛産業分野、量子分野、海洋分野の投資額は、増加しているものの依然として小規模だ。例
えば、量子分野は研究開発～実証段階の技術が中心の新領域であり、分野の将来的な成長期待は大き
い。現時点では収益化まで距離があることから、投資額の小ささ自体が問題とは言えないものの、米
国等と比べると日本の産業基盤は相対的に小さく、 スタートアップ企業の集積でも遅れを取っている。
さらに投資を呼び込むうえでは、こうした遅れを埋める手立てを講じることが必要不可欠であろう。 
また、造船分野では、2020年代に入ってから中国の存在感が一段と高まっている。日本の造船会社
による船舶の新規受注量は2000年代半ばから減少しており、世界の船舶建造量に占める日本のシェア
も低下傾向だ。背景には、中国と比べた事業所規模の小ささが指摘されており、生産コストなどの面
で水をあけられている。 
■ 幅広い分野での外国資本取り込みに向けて供給面 ・ 制度面のボトルネック軽減が重要 
戦略分野全体でみれば投資流入の堅調さが確認できた一方で、17の戦略分野それぞれにおいて投資
流入増を実現していく1ためには、 投資の門戸を開くだけでは不十分だ。 造船分野や量子分野に関して
指摘したような課題の解決に向けては、ボトルネックを軽減する地道な取り組みが必要であろう。ま
た、足元で急速に拡大しているデータセンター投資や再エネ関連投資についても、今後は成長の維持
が課題となる。 
外国資本取り込みにおける具体的なボトルネックとして、人材不足等の供給制約や複雑な制度、言
語障壁が挙げられる。日本国内の外資系企業を対象にした、日本貿易振興機構による「2025年度外資
系企業ビジネス実態調査」 では、 直近1～2年の日本のビジネス環境の課題について、 多くの企業が 「高
度人材等の確保」 と 「一般人材の確保」 を回答しているほか、 直近では 「オフィス・事業用地の確保」
図表5 戦略17分野別にみた国内供給力強化投資額の推移

（注） 各社資料で確認できなかった具体的な投資額はfDi Marketsによる推計値。 期間内に国内供給力強化投資が確認できなかった分野につい
ては除外。各社の個別投資プロジェクトの情報及び、日本成長戦略会議資料を基に、みずほ総合研究所調査部が分類

4

を回答する企業の割合も高まっている。こうした人材・立地面等の供給制約は、外資の取り込みを進
めるうえで課題となる。 
また、制度面について、日本貿易振興機構による「ジェトロ対日投資報告2018」では、外国企業が
日本でビジネス展開するうえでの阻害要因として「行政手続きの複雑さ」や「許認可制度の厳しさ」
等が挙げられ、外国企業にとって日本のビジネス環境への適応が容易ではないことがうかがえる。な
お、 同調査では 「外国語によるコミュニケーションの難しさ」 が2位にランクするなど、 言語障壁も外
国企業の参入を阻む一因だ。 
供給面・制度面のボトルネック解消に向けた対応として、人材不足については、大学等の研究・教
育機関との連携強化を通じた国内人材の育成・確保や、高度外国人材の受入強化による対応が考えら
れる。また、用地不足に対しては、産業用地の確保や空き家・空き地の有効活用を通じて、外国企業
が拠点を設立しやすい環境を整備することが重要だろう。 
制度面の対応としては、まず、手続きの一本化が挙げられる。現状、外国企業等の投資申請に際し
ては担当省庁・公的機関と個別にやり取りするケースがあり、外国企業の手続き負担は小さいとは言
えない。 この点、OECD（2026） は外国企業 ・ 投資家と関係省庁等との調整を担う 「ワンストップ窓口」
の機能強化を提言している。 言語障壁については近年通訳や英訳による手続き支援が進みつつあるが、
主要な政策等については英語アナウンスを適時提供するなど、言語障壁軽減に向けたさらなる取り組
みが求められる。

［参考文献］ 
OECD (2022), FDI Qualities Policy Toolkit, OECD Publishing 
OECD (2025), OECD Benchmark Definition of Foreign Direct Investment,  5th edition, OECD 
Publishing 
OECD (2026), OECD Economic Surveys: Japan 2026, OECD Publishing

### Page 5

5

1 コンテンツ産業のような日本企業に競争力がある分野では、外国資本の取り込みによる事業拡大も期待される一方、独自技術
や知的財産の流出リスクには留意し、慎重に議論を進める必要がある。

⚫ 8 月美國零售與餐飲服務銷售額 7,739 億美元，月增 1.2%、年增 6.0%，
扭轉前月下滑走勢；核心零售(扣除汽車、零件及加油站)月增 1.2%。其
中非實體零售商、加油站及電子家電商店銷售成長，顯示美國消費需求
仍具支撐力道。

2.7%；新屋開工降至 127.5 萬戶，月減2.6%，惟單戶住宅開工升至 91.8
萬戶 、 創近5 個月新高 。 另一方面 ， 在建住宅年減3.2%、 完工年減27.1%，
顯示高利率與需求不確定性仍抑制整體住宅建設活動。 
⚫ 9 月聯邦公開市場委員會(FOMC)決議以 12 比 0 一致升息 1 碼至 3.75%
至 4.00%，為 2023 年 7 月以來首度升息。委員會認為美國經濟及勞動
市場仍具韌性，惟通膨持續高於 2%目標，因此採取升息行動並重申致

國際經濟金融週報 
兆豐國際商業銀行 徵信處 
115.9.21 
一、 美國—消費回暖、房市降溫，Fed 政策轉向與川習會成市場焦點 
2026 年 8 月美國零售與餐飲服務銷售額經季節性調整後為 7,739 億美元，較
上月增加 1.2%，優於 7 月修正後月減 0.5%的表現，年增率亦達 6.0%，顯示美國
消費支出動能於 8 月明顯回升。若扣除汽車及零件銷售，零售額月增1.4%、年增
6.9%；進一步扣除汽車、零件及加油站後，核心零售銷售月增1.2%、年增 5.6%，
反映核心消費需求維持穩健成長。從主要業別表現觀察，非實體零售商銷售額月
增 2.6%、加油站月增 3.1%、電子及家電商店月增 1.6%，食品服務及飲酒場所亦
月增 1.2%，顯示線上消費、能源相關支出及服務性消費持續成長；另一方面，建
築材料與園藝用品經銷商月減 0.2%，對整體零售表現形成部分抵銷。整體而言，
8 月美國零售銷售由前月下滑轉為明顯成長，且扣除汽車及能源相關項目後仍維
持穩健增幅，顯示美國消費需求仍具支撐力道，對經濟活動形成正面挹注，惟後
續仍須持續關注物價變動、金融情勢及消費信心對零售支出的影響。 
2026 年 8 月美國住宅市場持續呈現分化走勢，建築許可年化總數降至 139.4
萬戶，月減 2.7%，惟較去年同期增加 3.5%；其中單戶住宅建築許可 87.8 萬戶，
月減 1.8%，5 戶以上多戶住宅建築許可 46.7 萬戶，月減 3.1%，顯示住宅開發規
劃動能較前月略有降溫 。 另一方面 ， 新屋開工年化數降至127.5 萬戶 ， 月減2.6%、
年減 1.2%，其中單戶住宅開工回升至 91.8 萬戶，月增 7.6%、年增 5.2%，為近 5
個月最高水準 ； 惟5 戶以上多戶住宅開工降至 34.4 萬戶 ， 月減22.5%、 年減15.5%，
拖累整體開工表現。施工方面，在建住宅年化數為 127.1 萬戶，月增 0.3%，但較
去年同期減少 3.2%，顯示住宅建設規模仍呈收斂趨勢；完工年化數則降至 112.8
萬戶，月減 11.9%、年減 27.1%，反映住宅供給落成速度明顯放緩。整體而言，8
月美國住宅市場呈現單戶住宅需求相對具支撐、但多戶住宅及整體營建活動偏弱
的格局，顯示在高利率與需求前景仍具不確定性的環境下，建商投資態度仍趨審
慎，後續仍須關注利率走勢及房市需求變化對住宅市場的影響。

下：(一)儘管地緣政治風險推升經濟前景不確定性，美國經濟活動仍維持穩健擴

最新 SEP 顯示，2026 年實質 GDP 成長率預測中位數由 2.2%上修至 2.3%，失業
率由 4.3%下修至 4.1%；PCE 及核心 PCE 通膨率則分別上修至 3.7%及 3.4%，顯

升至 4.1%，2027 年底維持 4.1%，官員仍保留進一步升息可能，整體政策立場較
6 月轉趨鷹派；(五)主席 Kevin Warsh 表示，美國經濟與勞動市場維持韌性，通膨
仍高於目標且地緣政治風險升溫 ， 為本次升息主因 。 未來聯準會不提供前瞻指引 ，
也不預設利率路徑，將依經濟數據與展望機動調整政策。 
在 9 月 24 日美國華府白宮舉行之 「川習會」 前 ， 美國財長貝森特(Scott Bessent)
與中國國務院副總理何立峰於紐約摩根大通總部舉行會談，美國貿易代表葛里爾
(Jamieson Greer) 亦共同參與。雙方將就人工智慧 (AI)、關稅、貿易休戰及關鍵礦
產供應等議題交換意見，並為川普與習近平會晤預作準備。討論重點包括將於11
月 10 日到期之美中貿易休戰安排、中國稀土磁材及關鍵礦產出口供應情況，以
及 AI 模型安全與治理機制等議題。此外，雙方亦將檢視今年 5 月北京會談後尚
待落實之事項，包括降低部分非戰略性商品關稅、中國增加美國農產品採購及波
音客機採購計畫等。市場普遍預期本次會談將以維持既有合作架構及避免貿易緊
張升級為主要目標，短期內出現重大突破的可能性仍相對有限，美中關稅、關鍵
礦產供應及 AI 治理議題預料將成為本次川習會的重要焦點。 
美國政府近期在美墨加協定(USMCA)檢討談判中，要求墨西哥針對人工智慧
(AI)伺服器、半導體及相關硬體產品建立更嚴格的原產地規範，擬限制來自中國
等非北美地區零組件占比，並可能參照汽車產業的區域含量標準，以防堵外國企
業透過墨西哥組裝後出口美國，規避美國對中國商品實施的關稅與貿易限制。市
場分析指出，在美國 AI 基礎建設需求帶動下，墨西哥已成為重要 AI 伺服器生產

基地，今年上半年 AI 資料中心伺服器出口金額達 830 億美元，其中 94%銷往美
國，並超越汽車成為對美最大出口項目。由於鴻海等台灣電子代工廠在墨西哥設
有 AI 伺服器生產據點，若相關規範正式上路，供應鏈可能被迫提高北美在地採
購及生產比重，進一步重塑北美 AI 硬體供應鏈布局。美墨雙方預計 9 月底於華
府展開新一輪談判，惟相關原產地門檻、過渡期間及適用範圍尚未定案。 
美國總統川普已於 9 月 18 日簽署《2026 年 Lindsey O. Graham 制裁俄羅斯與
伊朗法案》，該法案此前分別以參議院 86 票對 11 票、眾議院 262 票對 159 票通
過。法案除擴大對俄羅斯政府官員、金融機構及協助能源運輸的「影子艦隊」制
裁外，亦授權總統對持續大量購買俄羅斯石油及天然氣的第三國商品課徵最高
100%關稅，其中中國、印度等俄羅斯能源主要買家可能受到影響。法案目的在於
削弱俄羅斯能源出口收入及其支撐烏俄戰爭的財政來源，並強化對伊朗的相關制
裁。 
二、歐元區—美歐矛盾與歐中貿易協商在即，增添工業復甦變數 
 歐元區 7 月工業生產較 6 月下滑 0.1%，為連續兩個月衰退，其中非耐久消費
財產出減少 1.6%，抵銷中間財、資本財、消費耐久財分別成長0.3%、0.5%、0.9%
的影響，主要是美伊衝突帶來的庫存儲備需求持續消退，抵銷德國軍備採購及消
暑用品消費增加的影響。以國別而言，德國工業生產下跌1.5%最為疲軟，且為該
國近一年最大跌幅，主要是部分汽車大廠決定提前進行歲修，故預期待歲修結束
後，8、9 月份可望出現汽車庫存回補的生產需求，此外，在德國軍備採購需求的
支持下 ， 德國7 月工業訂單仍大幅成長 2.5%， 相關產出可望在第三季末及第四季
陸續反映；法、義、西工業生產月增率則分別為-0.4%、0.7%、0.6%。 
 整體而言，美伊衝突爆發後，能源密集產業普遍增產，但該類產業從 6 月起
已有較明顯的趨緩跡象，其他主要產業則大致呈現橫向波動，顯示工業生產趨勢
仍屬溫和。惟近期歐盟提議讓加拿大成為歐盟準會員國，引發美國不滿，可能進
一步升高美歐貿易與外交摩擦；此外，歐盟與中國預定於 10 月 8 日針對貿易失
衡問題進行重要協商 ， 後續若涉及貿易措施調整 ， 亦可能影響歐元區製造業表現 。 
三、日本—央行升息 1 碼，12 月仍有再升息空間 
7 月核心機械訂單月減 3.7%，其中製造業訂單小幅下滑，主要受到化學
產業訂單減少影響，惟電子產品及電氣機械訂單持續成長，反映人工智慧相

關產業及供應鏈企業仍積極擴充產能，支撐資本支出動能。 雖然非製造業訂
單則因資通訊及資訊服務業需求回落影響而表現較弱，但在人力短缺與數位
轉型需求支撐下，投資動能仍具韌性。綜合來看，企業獲利穩健及AI 投資熱
潮延續，預期日本企業資本支出仍將維持成長態勢。 
8 月出口年增 19.3%，半導體相關產品仍為主要成長動能，其中半導體等
電子零件出口大幅成長 52.3%。從地區來看，對美國、中國及歐盟出口分別
成長 24.9%、20.6%及 11.0%，半導體等電子零組件對中國出口金額，以及對
美國 、 歐盟的半導體製造設備出口均較去年同期倍增，顯示全球 AI 及高科技
產業投資需求持續支撐日本出口表現。 
8 月全國消費者物價指數(CPI)年增 1.9%，主要受到政府恢復電力與天然
氣補貼，以及米價回落抑制食品價格所致。從細項來看，石油產品價格上漲
帶動家庭消費品等核心商品價格續漲；服務類通膨除通訊費用趨穩外，其他
服務項目已開始出現漲價跡象。展望未來，能源成本上升及企業將 薪資成本
逐步轉嫁至售價，預料將推動通膨壓力由商品擴散至更多服務領域，使通膨
維持緩步升溫趨勢，並進一步支持央行持續推動貨幣政策正常化。 
日本央行於 9 月 17、18 日召開貨幣政策會議，宣布將政策利率調升 1 碼
至 1.25%。雖然政策聲明整體基調變化不大，但仍持續強調通膨上行風險，
並指出目前金融條件仍相對寬鬆。 值得關注的是 ， 本次決策出現兩張反對票 ，
顯示委員會內部對經濟與通膨前景看法分歧加劇，一派認為當前經濟與通膨
動能尚不足以支持升息，另一派則認為通膨升溫風險高於預期。在會後記者
會上，總裁植田和男指出，日本貨幣政策已進入新的階段，由過去較重視通
膨低於目標的風險，轉為兼顧通膨上、下行風險，並強調未來升息步調將視
經濟與物價發展而定，不會預先承諾特定路徑。此外，他也表示 主要國家央
行升息對日本匯率及金融環境具有重要影響，因此將持續納入政策考量。雖
然總裁未透露最終利率水準，但相關談話暗示目前 1.25%的政策利率可能尚
未達到中性水準，日本央行亦未排除未來進一步升息的可能性。整體而言，
日本央行對通膨前景看法趨於樂觀，政策立場仍偏向緊縮，預期若未來數季
通膨持續走揚，日本央行仍有望於 12 月再度升息。

四、中國—8 月內需持續疲乏，與歐美的關係將為近期關注重點 
在內需方面，1-8 月累計零售額年增率較 2025 年同期增加 1.1%，較 1-7 月累
計的 1.2%持續下降。其中 8 月零售額年增率放緩 0.2 個百分點至 0.4%，反映以
舊換新的補貼力道不如過去兩年水準，加上房市低迷，以及對景氣波動較為敏感
的消費耐久財需求進一步下滑 ，8 月家具 、 汽車 、 裝潢消費分別年減7.9%、18.5%、
11.8%。另外，1-8 月固定資產投資年減幅由 6.7%擴大至 7.2%，反映美伊衝突、
政府主導去化過剩產能、美國貿易戰等因素導致民間投資需求急遽萎縮；萎縮產
業包含化工、醫藥、專用設備、汽車製造業、金屬製品業等。同期間房地產開發
投資額年減幅由 19.2%擴大至 19.9%、施工面積年減幅則擴大為 13.2%，新建商
品房銷售面積年減幅擴大為 13%，房市投資、買氣皆持續低迷。 
受到出口維持高度成長帶動 ， 抵銷內需疲軟的影響 ，8 月工業生產年增率較 7
月回升 0.7 個百分點至 5.2%(1-8 月工業生產年增率持平 1-7 月水準為 5.3%)。依
產業別來看，8 月份高科技產品仍是主要成長動能，包含 IC(成長 20.6%)、工業
機器人(34.6%)、服務機器人(5%)、 新能源車(21.9%)等 ； 惟受到原油供應減少以及
能源價格上漲壓力影響，化纖、水泥、玻璃、鋼材等產量持續衰退，另因 3C 關
鍵物料漲價影響，手機產量衰退幅度高達 22.3%，太陽能電池則因取消出口退稅
政策跌幅高達 12.9%。展望未來，若9 月 24 日川習會有正向發展，美中關稅休戰
得以延長 ， 且歐中貿易摩擦未進一步惡化 ， 將有助於穩定中國出口與製造業景氣 ，
抵銷內需疲軟的經濟壓力。

五、台灣—央行維持利率不變並適度鬆綁不動產信用管制措施 
台灣央行於 9 月 17 日召開理監事會議，決議維持政策利率(重貼現率)於 2%
不變，央行指出，雖然國際油價維持高檔、服務類通膨仍具黏性，但國內通膨仍
屬可控範圍，2026 年 1 至 8 月通膨年增 1.84%，預估 2027 年可回落至 2%以下；
同時考量中東地緣政治、美國經貿政策及 AI 產業發展等不確定因素，因此採取
審慎觀望態度，暫不升息。 
此外，央行認為先前實施之選擇性信用管制已逐漸見效，房市交易降溫、不
動產貸款集中度下降，因此適度調整相關措施，包括將自然人第二戶購屋貸款最
高成數由 6 成提高至 7 成，並刪除購地貸款須切結於一定期間內動工興建之規
定，改由金融機構依授信實務審慎評估辦理。整體而言，本次政策方向呈現利率
維持審慎偏緊基調、房市管制微幅鬆綁。

## Document 152: 8月金融数据点评：直接融资累计占比首破50%

Source PDF: all report pdf/8月金融数据点评：直接融资累计占比首破50%.pdf
Report source: 中证鹏元
Date: 2026-09-27
Pages: 14

### Page 1

中证鹏元资信评估股份有限公司
研究发展部
赵云蛟

吴进辉

主要内容：
9 月 14 日，央行公布 2026 年 8 月金融统计数据，社融增量大幅收缩，宏观融资结
构迎来历史性拐点，直接融资累计占比首次突破 50%。社融方面，表内信贷与政府
债高基数构成主要拖累，存量增速持续向下突破，融资结构正逐步摆脱对传统表内
信贷的单一依赖。信贷方面居民与企业需求双弱，总量延续弱势，当前微观主体加
杠杆意愿依然不足，资产负债表的自发修复与收缩仍在深化。居民端长短贷同步净
偿还，企业短贷延续收缩，中长贷在大规模设备更新及基建项目资本金落地驱动下
表现相对韧性。货币方面，M2 同比 7.5%，M1 微升至 4.1%，剪刀差小幅收敛。信
贷收缩拖累 M2 探底，财政资金拨付支撑 M1 边际企稳。存款整体增量偏弱，财政
发债资金加速拨付向实体账户转移并形成阶段性支撑，但低息环境下资金向理财等
非银体系分流的“存款出表”趋势依然存在。

整体而言，金融数据总体偏弱，当前经济仍然处于新旧动能转换与结构性再平衡阶
段，分化特征突出。内需仍然较弱，传统依赖高杠杆主体的信用扩张路径正在解构。
前期集中发行的政府债落地转化为项目资金并加快形成实物工作量，叠加 9 月新型
政策性金融工具开始实质性投放，预计相关配套资金落地将在 9 月社融的委托贷款
数据中率先显现，并对四季度乃至明年年初的企业中长期贷款形成跨周期支撑。月
末出台的“828”地产一揽子政策可能在四季度对信贷两端形成边际托底，按揭成
本及购房门槛优化有助于改善购房预期并部分缓解提前还贷压力，政策对房企融资
环境的改善配合白名单机制推进或将为企业中长贷企稳提供协同动能。后续重点关
注财政金融协同促内需增量政策出台的可能性及政策效果。

### Page 3

2026 年 9 月 14 日，央行公布 2026 年 8 月金融统计数据。
一、社融：8 月社融增速延续下滑，直接融资累计占比首破 50%
2026 年 8 月新增社融 1.66 万亿元，同比少增 9,041 亿元；社融存量增速较上月回落 0.2 个百分点至 7.2%，
进一步下探。本月社融同比规模的大幅回落，主要受表内信贷深度收缩以及去年同期政府债高基数的双重
压制。
从当月分项来看，单月增量的主要贡献来自于政府债与企业债，信贷仍然是核心拖累。具体而言，今
年上半年政府债整体发行偏慢，7 月逆季节性集中发力后，8 月发行节奏环比出现回落；叠加去年同期处于
下半年单月发行峰值的高基数效应，当月政府债券新增 1.01 万亿元，同比少增 3,544 亿元。尽管发行规模
较前期及往年同期有所下降，但单月绝对增量连续两个月维持在万亿级别以上，依然是支撑广义融资总量
的核心分项。同时，实体企业加速通过债券市场进行低息替代，8 月企业债券新增 2,687 亿元，同比大幅多
增 1,349 亿元。相较之下，间接融资延续收缩，8 月向实体经济发放的人民币贷款仅新增 591 亿元，同比大

幅多减 5,662 亿元，但较 7 月负值回正；表外未贴现承兑汇票新增 383 亿元，同比少增 1,590 亿元。
结合年内累计数据观察，宏观融资结构正迎来历史性拐点。1-8 月新增社融中，广义直接融资（政府债、
企业债及股票融资）累计占比首次突破 50%，达 50.3%，而表内信贷（人民币贷款、外币贷款）累计占比
则加速收缩至 43.7%。直接融资占比超越表内信贷占据主导，主要由两方面逻辑驱动：一方面是间接融资的
被动收缩。在央行严查资金空转、引导信贷“降速提质”的导向下，前期依赖票据和低效贷款冲量的信贷
“水分”被持续挤出，叠加传统高杠杆主体扩张意愿不足，信贷增速面临趋势性放缓；另一方面是直接融
资的主动扩张。政府部门加杠杆成为广义融资的主引擎，同时实体企业在低利率环境下自发优化债务结构，
加速向直接融资渠道倾斜。直接融资占比的历史性突破，进一步说明单一信贷指标已难以全面表征当前的
宏观融资条件。

二、信贷：总量延续弱势，企业中长贷表现相对韧性
2026 年 8 月新增人民币贷款仅 600 亿元，同比少增 5,300 亿元，增量大幅弱于历年同期季节性水平。
信贷总量极度偏弱，当前微观主体加杠杆意愿依然不足，资产负债表的自发修复与收缩仍在深化。
从分项来看，居民端贷款减少 2,029 亿元，长短端同步净偿还。其中，中长贷减少 822 亿元，当月按揭
贷款派生仍受制于地产销售低迷，且居民主动降杠杆、提前还贷的微观财务决策仍在延续；短贷减少 1,219
亿元，反映出当前居民收入预期偏审慎，消费信贷等短期扩张动能收敛，后续重点关注财政金融协同促内
需政策发力效果对居民短贷的影响。
企业端贷款新增 2,600 亿元，同比少增 3,300 亿元。在整体信贷偏弱的格局下，企业中长贷新增 3,200

### Page 7

亿元，表现出相对韧性，成为本月表内信贷的主要支撑项。企业长贷的边际企稳主要受政策端及重大项目
驱动：一方面，在大规模设备更新等政策引导下，制造业相关领域的配套中长期融资需求得到一定支撑；
另一方面，7-8 月政府债集中发行后，重大基建项目资本金逐步到位，银行配套的长端信贷随之跟进。企业
短贷延续负增长，减少 1,600 亿元，表明在宏观总需求偏弱、企业盈利预期受限及投资回报率承压的背景下，
实体企业常规营运资金的扩张意愿审慎。此外，票据融资新增 1,000 亿元，在防资金空转监管下，票据贴现
虚增规模的空间正在被实质性压缩。
月末出台的“828”地产一揽子政策由于存在时滞，对 8 月当月数据尚未产生直接影响，但其政策效应
有望在四季度对信贷两端形成边际托底。在居民端，按揭成本及购房门槛的优化，有助于改善购房预期并
部分缓解提前还贷压力，进而对居民中长贷形成支撑；在企业端，政策对房企融资环境的改善，配合房地
产项目白名单融资机制的推进，也将为后续企业中长贷的企稳提供协同动能。

三、存款及货币供应：信贷收缩拖累 M2 阶段性探底，财政资金拨付支撑 M1 边际企稳
8 月，M1 同比 4.1%，较上月微升 0.1 个百分点，M2 同比 7.5%，较上月回落 0.2 个百分点。同时，社
融存量同比 7.2%，较上月回落 0.2 个百分点。
从货币供给结构看，M2 增速连降 3 个月至 7.5%，创 2025 年二季度以来新低，M1 同比微升至 4.1%，
带动 M2-M1 剪刀差小幅收敛至 3.4%。M2 的持续下滑主要原因是表内信贷萎缩对存款派生形成拖累，而
M1 的边际企稳得益于前期政府债资金加速拨付补充了企业活期现金流。同时，8 月社融与 M2 增速同步回
落 0.2 个百分点，社融与 M2 剪刀差倒挂幅度维持在-0.3%，持平上月，两者的同步回落均由表内信贷的深
度收缩主导。M2 下行直接受制于信贷派生机制受阻，社融增速回落也主要由人民币贷款大幅少增拖累，政
府债的高基数效应进一步放大了总体降幅。尽管单月剪刀差倒挂幅度企稳，但较上半年已显著收窄，在信

贷“降速提质”的背景下，广义融资正逐步降低对传统表内信贷的单一依赖。
存款方面，8 月新增人民币存款 1.2 万亿元，同比少增 8,600 亿元。其中，居民存款与企业存款增量均
弱于去年同期，而非银机构存款逆势增加 5,600 亿元。8 月财政存款仅增加 1,100 亿元，同比少增 800 亿元。
结合当月政府债券净融资超万亿的绝对规模来看，财政存款并未出现大幅堆积，印证了 7 月以来集中发行
的政府债资金在 8 月已加快拨付使用。财政资金由国库向实体项目账户转移，在边际上补充了企业的活期
现金流，成为本月 M1 增速微升的支撑因素。但受制于信贷收缩与低息环境，企业闲置资金加速向理财等非
银渠道分流，导致企业存款依然偏弱，而非银存款延续扩张，“存款出表”趋势持续深化。
综合而言，当前广义流动性扩张受制于实体信贷需求收缩，广义流动性的维持与资金活化正愈发依赖
于政府加杠杆后的财政支出节奏与资金落地效率。

## Document 176: A changing investment narrative requires a careful reader (2026-10-05)

Source PDF: all report pdf/A changing investment narrative requires a careful reader (2026-10-05).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#australia #luciellis #interestrates #businessconditions #credit
A changing investment narrative requires a careful reader
By Luci Ellis
Chief Economist Westpac Group, Westpac
10:43 September 18 2026 Share
Business investment is now in the driver’s seat on growth, implying credit growth is a less
reliable guide to financial conditions more broadly.
Strong business investment, autonomous from government or central bank policy, has filled the gap
left by slower growth in public demand, and changed the narrative around the growth cycle. This has

implications for how we should think about financial conditions, and specifically business credit

### Page 2

Historically, much of the commentary on the growth cycle in Australia has been policy-driven. The RBA
sets the cash rate, which affects housing and mortgage markets, and so consumer spending. Business
investment was seen as largely downstream of a consumer-driven domestic demand cycle, on the logic
that businesses will not invest if demand is not there.

There were exceptions to this, most notably the rise of China and associated mining investment boom.
The pervasive effects of this boom and its unwind were systematically underestimated. Also not fully
appreciated at the time was the deadening effect of risk aversion and weak investment appetite in the
wake of the GFC. But these factors mostly played out over a longer horizon, and the shorter-run rates–
consumer nexus tended to be more in focus.

Another policy-driven narrative became more prominent in recent years, with public demand as the
swing variable. Government infrastructure projects and the growth in the care economy were driving
growth in domestic demand. Initially we worried that when the infrastructure projects ended and the
expansion in the care economy matured, the handover would be ‘shaky’ and growth momentum would
weaken further.

Fortunately, that did not happen. Developments in the private sector, autonomous from domestic policy,
have recently become a key driver and filled the gap as public sector demand growth slowed. In many
respects, the data centre boom and associated renewables spend is rewriting the narrative, and our
recent forecast revisions reflect this.

This shift has several implications for how we should think about financial conditions and specifically
how business credit growth fits into any assessment of financial conditions.

First, business investment is not that correlated with short-term interest rates. This was true in
the post-GFC period of slow growth, when hurdle rates stayed high even though interest rates were
unusually low. It will also be true in the upswing. The hyperscalers and other funders that are driving
the data centre boom are essentially indifferent to cost. They do not seem to care that they are funding
themselves at much wider spreads than similarly rated non-tech firms. Nor do they seem to care about
cost to build. They just want to get it done as fast as possible. But if their activities are impervious to
growth.
Typically, business investment is only loosely correlated with interest rates, and business credit
growth is in turn only loosely correlated with new investment. Business balance sheets strengthened
in the wake of the pandemic, so there might be scope for further increases in leverage. The pre-
pandemic period of low growth, inflation and interest rates is in any case not the relevant period of
comparison for where we are now.
If something is essentially impervious to interest rates, then it cannot be an indicator of financial
conditions more broadly. There is a difference between a claim that policy is restrictive but needs to
be even tighter because of a positive shock, and a claim that policy is not tight at all. These are
important considerations for how we should interpret developments in business credit.

### Page 3

interest rates and spreads, they cannot guide views on how the economy is responding to interest
rates.

Second, business investment is only loosely correlated with business credit quarter to quarter or
year to year. A stylised description of many businesses’ strategies is to use debt funding to acquire
assets or other companies, but use retained earnings to fund new investment. Short-run fluctuations in
business credit growth might therefore provide weak or misleading signals about investment or activity
more broadly. Moreover, if businesses’ funding mix changes, even medium-term shifts in business
credit growth might have little implication for underlying activity.

Third, balance sheets matter. Businesses came out of COVID cashed up, in part because of the fiscal
support they received during the pandemic. Business leverage – whether measured as debt relative to
financial assets broadly or debt to cash plus deposits – dipped below even the low post-GFC average.
It has since returned to that average but might have scope to increase further. This would not
necessarily mean credit supply has eased. Rather, the population of borrowers has effectively changed
and become stronger.

Fourth, and related to the previous point, the comparison period matters. We must recall that the
period between the GFC and the pandemic was the anomaly – low growth, low inflation, low
investment – and extremely low interest rates. The global structure of interest rates is likely to be higher
now and into the future, because of the AI boom, the US fiscal situation and the end of the post-GFC
inflation undershoot. But this is not news, and it is not necessarily a sign of a change in financial
conditions more recently.

This point is particularly salient for total credit growth as an indicator of financial conditions. Even a
return to inflation around or above target, rather than consistently below as in the 2010s, means that
average nominal credit growth rates from that period are not representative of “normal”. Nominal
growth in the economy was slower, and nominal credit growth is downstream from that. Moreover,
many of the factors that made the post-GFC period unusual – the weak risk appetite, regulatory
adjustments and balance sheet repair – would be expected to weigh on credit growth especially
heavily. There is also a cyclical element here: more (real) credit growth might not be saying anything
about credit supply, and so “financial conditions”. Rather, stronger growth makes for stronger borrowers
that are more willing to demand credit and more qualified to access it.

All of this suggests that observers should be cautious in how they interpret business credit conditions.
If something is essentially impervious to interest rates, then it cannot be an indicator of
financial conditions more broadly. To be fair, the hyperscalers are issuing bonds, not borrowing from
the domestic banking system and being recorded in business credit. To the extent that current business
investment and credit growth are being driven by the data centre boom’s broader spillovers to other
sectors, though, that provides almost no signal about the tightness of monetary policy.

### Page 4

Related articles
This is important given the apparent tendency of some recent RBA and other commentary to conflate a
shock with a reason to revise the neutral rate higher. For example, in one recent speech, it was argued
that, because AI was a smaller shock for Australia than some other countries (debateable), long yields
would rise by less than elsewhere. This in turn would result in a lower exchange rate than otherwise,
necessitating higher interest rates. But the exchange rate depreciation is part of the transmission of
that differential response to different-sized shocks. It is not an independent outcome requiring that
interest rates need to be higher, actually. In the face of a positive shock, yes, policy rates would need to
be higher than if the shock had not occurred. But being a lesser beneficiary of that shock is not a
heads-I-hike-tails-your-rates-go-up double-whammy.

This framing is even more curious, when one considers that the RBA never took any signal from the
USD selloff earlier in the year. That selloff had nothing to do with conditions in Australia, but it surely
lifted the AUD exchange rate at the margin – at least against some trading partners – and therefore
was slightly disinflationary.

The broader lesson here is not that this time is different, but rather “last time” – the period between the
GFC and the pandemic – was different. It has also taken a while to normalise from the pandemic itself,
especially where balance sheets and financial behaviour are concerned. If we fail to understand this
recovery away from the post-GFC era, or the implications of more fleeting shocks for financial
behaviour, we risk misconstruing where the economy might be headed and where policy settings need
to be to achieve desired outcomes. The strength of business credit does not have the same
implications as similarly strong household credit would.

The boom in data centres and renewables is reshaping Australia’s economic narrative and needs to be
a consideration in policy settings. But care must be taken to avoid overstating its implications for
financial conditions. There are many good reasons to think that the long-run average for the global
structure of interest rates (and so neutral rates) will be higher in future than pre-pandemic. Current
strong growth in business credit is not necessarily one of them.

## Document 181: AI, Ageing and the Budget_ Five Takeaways from the 2026 IGR (2026-10-05)

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI, Ageing and the Budget_ Five Takeaways from the 2026 IGR (2026-10-05).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 15
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#businessconditions #confidence
AI, Ageing and the Budget: Five Takeaways from the 2026 IGR
By Pat Bustamante
Senior Economist, Westpac
18:21 September 21 2026 Share
The 2026 Intergenerational Report is the 7th in a series of such reports, dating back to
2002. The IGR sets out a budget scenario highlighting potential budget challenges over
coming decades.

### Page 2

The familiar theme is that Australia’s ageing population will put upward pressure on expenditures and
downward pressure on revenues, creating a budget challenge.
In the 2026 edition, several forces are identified as continuing to shape Australia’s economy over the
coming decades: the artificial intelligence revolution; geopolitical fragmentation; energy transition;
ageing and the care economy; and Australia’s industrial transformation.
Below are the five key themes that stood out in this year's IGR: AI to be a game-changer for

productivity; Australians will live longer; NDIS reforms are crucial to budget sustainability, but knock on
to state budgets; Bracket creep has its limits, and future spending will need to be funded elsewhere;
and Higher costs of capital here to stay.
1. AI to be a game-changer for productivity
The IGR identifies AI as having the potential to be a game-changer for productivity growth. We agree
and have written extensively about the data centre and renewable energy investment surge. This

investment will boost value added and jobs in the near term. However, the real payoff will come when
the technology is widely adopted and businesses redesign processes to get the most out of AI.
The ultimate gains are difficult to estimate, in part because of the speed of development in the
technology. Another reason is that technological change at this scale does not simply augment or
automate existing roles but creates new roles, services and goods. The Productivity Commission

estimates that AI could boost multifactor productivity in Australia by at least 2.3 per cent over the next
decade, but this estimate was published in December, before the step-change in model capability we
saw early this year.
The IGR shows that productivity matters for the budget: productivity growth 0.4ppt higher than the
assumed 1.2% per year would reduce gross debt by 25ppts of GDP in 2065–66. Of course, this
assumes that fiscal policy does not respond to the faster trend growth that implies.

### Page 3

2. Australians will live longer
The IGR projects that life expectancy will continue to increase to 86.1 for males and 89.5 for females
by 2065, around five years longer than today. This presents both opportunities and challenges.
Older Australians are remaining in the workforce for longer, boosting labour supply and economic
activity. At the same time, rising demand for health, aged care and pension spending will place
increasing pressure on government budgets.

This is one area where AI, along with governance changes, could deliver outsized benefits. While an
ageing population will increase demand for government services, productivity-enhancing technologies
can improve service delivery and support prevention.
We know that the non-market sector (including health care, education and public administration) is the
largest it has ever been, with the expansion mainly driven by health services. Notwithstanding

measurement issues, we know that productivity levels in this sector has declined recently. Greater use
of evidence-based practice, preventive care, and AI-enabled technologies in health and aged care
could help improve productivity across this rapidly expanding part of the economy.

### Page 4

Demographic change will increase reliance on a smaller share of the population to fund the health,
aged care and other services required by a growing number of older Australians. We know that the
average fiscal contribution of an individual becomes negative later in life as government spending on
health and aged care rises. By contrast, people in their prime working years make the largest net
contribution to the budget through higher workforce participation and tax payments. 
This reinforces the importance of lifting productivity growth, both to support living standards and to help
meet rising spending pressures without placing an ever-greater burden on the working-age population.

3. NDIS reforms are crucial to budget sustainability, but knock on to state budgets 
The IGR highlights just how significant the NDIS reforms are for the fiscal outlook. Without the reforms,
payments would be around 1 percentage point of GDP higher over the medium term. The savings are
substantial and reinforce the importance of full implementation, a point we have previously made
(2026–27 Australian Federal Budget | Westpac IQ ). 
The report focuses on the Commonwealth budget, but the implications for state budgets could also be
significant. To the extent that some costs are shifted to states, a complete assessment of the reforms
requires a consolidated national fiscal perspective.

### Page 5

4. Bracket creep has its limits, and future spending will need to be funded elsewhere
Personal income tax receipts are projected to rise from 12.3% of GDP in 2025-26 to 14.1% of GDP by
2065-66, even with the Government capping tax as a share of the economy.
This highlights a structural challenge. As Australia ages, an increasing share of government revenue
will come from a relatively smaller working-age population, while the share of indirect taxes continues

to decline.
The IGR argues that relying ever more heavily on labour income taxation is unlikely to be a sustainable
long-run solution. Future governments may therefore need to look elsewhere for revenue growth. This
could include reforms to indirect taxes, particularly as fuel excise revenues come under pressure from
the transition to electric vehicles.
The report reinforces the need to modernise the tax base as activity shifts towards digital services and

intangible consumption. The revenue challenge is material, with the erosion of indirect tax bases
potentially amounting to close to 1ppt of GDP over coming decades, on top of the effective loss of
tobacco excise in recent years.
The terms of trade are assumed to remain broadly unchanged from their 2027–28 level, underpinning

### Page 6

company tax receipts. This bakes in the sharp near-term decline in iron ore prices that has been
assumed in every budget for more than a decade without eventuating. Despite this, the longer-run
trajectory is increasing uncertain as the world transitions towards net zero.
5. Higher costs of capital here to stay
We have long argued that the era of ultra-low interest rates is unlikely to return. The 2026 IGR
reinforces this view. Despite an improved fiscal position and lower projected debt than in the 2023 IGR,

interest payments are projected to be higher because government borrowing costs are assumed to
remain elevated relative to the post-GFC period.
Total interest payments are projected to rise from 0.9% of GDP in 2025–26 to 1.6% of GDP in 2032–33
before easing to 1.2% of GDP by 2065–66. Importantly, the primary cash balance (excluding interest
payments) is projected to remain in surplus until the mid-2050s. In other words, the budget is projected
to be in deficit largely because of the growing cost of servicing debt, rather than an underlying

imbalance between government revenues and expenditures.
The IGR’s sensitivity analysis highlights how important borrowing costs are for the fiscal outlook. If
bond yields average 5.4 per cent, broadly in line with current levels, the underlying cash deficit would
be around 0.5 percentage points of GDP larger and gross debt would be 6.6 percentage points of GDP
higher by 2065-66.

### Page 7

Related articles

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The Cotality home value index, covering all of Australia,
fell 1.1% in September with downward revisions to the
previous two months again shaving a further 0.4% off prices.
The correction has now seen prices decline 5.2% from their

March peak. Annual growth is flat.
The price correction remains broad, Cotality reporting 97% of
capital city suburbs experienced falls over the three months
to September, Darwin the only city to record gains. Sydney
and Melbourne continue to see more pronounced declines,
albeit moderating a touch, particularly in Melbourne.
Against this, declines in Brisbane, Adelaide and Perth have
accelerated over the last few months. Across segments, unit
prices and lower price tiers continue to track slightly milder

falls than houses and top tiers.
Seasonal variations accentuated price weakness through winter
but are now neutral. The peak-to-trough price decline nationally
is slightly milder at 4.6% in seasonally adjusted terms. Unless
specified, figures in titles, tables and the commentary below are
in non-seasonally adjusted terms, consistent with the ‘headline’
figures reported by Cotality. However, figures shown in charts
are in seasonally adjusted terms.

Preliminary estimates of turnover show rolling 3mth volumes
down 3.9%qtr in September and 17.4%yr with previous
estimates revised 1-2ppts lower. The monthly pattern
suggests small gains in July and August have given way to
another sizeable fall in September.
Looking at the price performance across different jurisdictions:
• Sydney prices dropped 1.4% fall in September, monthly
declines averaging 1.6% over the previous four months,
tracking a 17% annualised pace of decline since May. All

segments and tiers recorded falls in Q3 but with top and
middle tier houses notably weaker.
• Melbourne prices fell 0.7%mth, a slower pace than other
capitals. This likely reflects the weaker starting point coming
into the correction, prices already back to the levels seen in
mid-2021. As elsewhere, 'top tier' houses prices are leading
the decline. 'Low tier' unit prices mostly held their ground in
Q3, prices in this sub-segment already plumbing 10yr lows.

• Brisbane was the weakest capital city price-wise, recording
a 1.5% drop in September with previous monthly declines
also revised down heavily (August from 1% to 1.6% and
July from 1.2% to 1.7%). The combined effect has taken
the rolling 3mth change to –4.7%qtr from –2.7%qtr last
month and –0.6%qtr in July. At 5.9%yr, annual growth is
still positive but losing altitude quickly, having run at 18%yr
as recently as May. Sellers have been slow to respond to

the sudden stop with total listings up over 50% vs a year
ago, albeit still not high compared to long run averages
(reflecting the extremely tight starting point for the Brisbane
market).
• Adelaide dwelling prices fell 1.3%mth, the August decline

also marked down from 0.8% to 1.1%, annual price growth
dropping to 6.5%yr. The price correction began later in
Adelaide but is now running at a similar pace to prices
nationally.
• Perth recorded a 1.2% decline but also saw big downward
revisions to previous months (August from 0.8% to –1.7%
and July from –1.3% to –1.8%). At 10.1%yr, annual growth
is still solidly positive but was tracking at over 25%yr earlier
in the year. As with Adelaide and Brisbane, the detail shows

a more even spread of declines across price tiers. However,
turnover is showing a milder pull-back in Adelaide.
• The smaller capitals were again more mixed, price growth
still positive in Darwin (0.4%mth, 11.9%yr), relatively
resilient in Hobart (–0.5%mth, 7%yr) but weaker in
Canberra (–1.1%mth, –1.6%yr).
• Regional markets showed a 0.9% decline in Qld but slightly
milder declines in NSW (–0.7%) and Victoria (0.3%). Annual
prices growth is in the 3.4-6.3%yr range.

Our recently released Housing Pulse report (here) provides
a deeper diver into the situation in Australia's housing
markets. While the absence of selling pressure, still tight
supply-demand balance and sentiment mix suggests the
correction was still relatively 'orderly' through the September
quarter, the latest monthly data shows momentum remains
unambiguously negative.
That said, there are clearly more headwinds to come. The
RBA latest interest rate rise and what now looks to be a

more likely prospect of a follow-up increase at its meeting in
early November will impact in coming months. Next week's
consumer sentiment update will show how the change, and
the latest round of weak updates, is shaping housing-related
sentiment. Either way, momentum looks set to remain weak
into year-end and, barring a significantly improved inflation
and interest rate outlook, early 2027.

## Document 259: France - 2027 Budget_ Fiscal consolidation must now resume (2026-10-01)

Source PDF: all report pdf/France - 2027 Budget_ Fiscal consolidation must now resume (2026-10-01).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 1st October 2026

EcoFlash

2027 Budget: Fiscal consolidation must now resume

The 2027 budget, adopted by the French Council of Ministers today, calls for a reduction in France’s fiscal 
deficit to 5% of GDP in 2027 (down from 5.4% in 2026). This is a first step toward s the multi-year fiscal 
consolidation needed to stabilise the public debt-to-GDP ratio. This ratio is expected to rise further in 2027 
and until the deficit is reduced to 3% of GDP. Furthermore, given the increase in some expenditures (+0.6 pp 
of GDP for debt service and defence spending), the projected fiscal effort amounts to 1 pp. Nearly one-third 
of this effort would come from higher compulsory levies, and two -thirds from curbing government and 
social spending.

2027 Draft finance law: A step in the right direction. The government is forecasting an improvement in the fiscal 
deficit from 5.4% of GDP in 2026 to 5% in 2027. This projection is based on a credible growth assumption of 1% 
(see our analysis of French growth drivers). To achieve this target, compulsory levies (CL) are expected to rise to 
44.2% of GDP, an increase of 0.3 pp (the same increase as in 2026, compared with +0.9 pp in 2025). The public 
spending-to-GDP ratio would decline by 0.2 pp to 56.9%. The effort required to reduce the deficit to 5% is greater 
than it appears. Indeed, some expenditures will increase (interest payments by 0.4 pp and defence spending by 
0.2 pp). Consequently, the fiscal effort for next year amounts not to 0.4 pp, but to 1 pp of GDP. One-third of this will 
be achieved through increased CL, and two-thirds through spending cuts. Social spending will contribute, notably 
through the cancellation of EUR 3 billion in social contribution reductions for low-wage earners, as well as efforts 
totaling EUR 6 billion each in the areas of pensions and healthcare. Social spending shows the largest deviation 
from pre-COVID levels, with an increase representing 1.2 pp of GDP, nearly half of the gap between the 2026 
budget balance and a 3% deficit. Government spending, excluding interest payments and defence, is expected to 
be frozen in nominal terms.

A compromise seems likely despite a tight schedule. The French National Assembly will start reviewing the 
budget on Tuesday 13 October. The government will need to ensure that a majority vote against the draft budget 
does not form. The current circumstances, including increasing sovereign bond yields and the upcoming 
presidential election, call for a consensus based on fiscal consolidation of the size proposed by the government. If 
Parliament does not pass a budget by the end of the year, the government will have to choose between 
implementing the government’s budget proposal by executive order ( only possible if there was no parliamentary 
vote) or through a special law (as in 2025 and 2026). A special law would enable tax collection , while the 
government and social security agencies could continue to issue debt. In this case, the 2026 budget would apply 
in 2027 (without indexation) until Parliament passes a budget in the first few weeks of the year. This must occur 
before the end of February (when Parliament’s session ends, earlier than usual due to the presidential election).

The one-year postponement of the 5% budget deficit target has a measurable impact. The gap between the 
budget adopted for 2026 (deficit of 5% of GDP) and the actual budget outcome (deficit of 5.4%) is due to 
unforeseeable circumstances (the conflict in Iran  and extreme drought). However, postponing the deficit target  
previously set for 2026 to 2027 comes at a cost: 1) the public debt-to-GDP ratio is expected to reach 121% in 2027 
according to our forecasts (1 pp above our scenario with a 5% target in 2026 and a deficit reduced to 4.5% in 2027), 
and 2) the (upcoming) postponement to 2032 of the 3% of GDP deficit target (a commitment that most presidential 
candidates plan to make) rather than in 2029 (the government had committed to this with the European Commission 
in 2024). In 2032, the public debt-to-GDP ratio would stabilise at 124% of GDP (instead of 121.5% if the deficit had 
been reduced to 5% of GDP as early as 2026 and to 3% of GDP by the end of the decade).

Another delay in fiscal consolidation would further erode France’s credibility. This new delay could stem 
either from a decision by the government formed in May 2027 to immediately implement part of its agenda, or from 
the enactment of a special law that would remain in effect until (just after) the likely legislative elections in June  
2027  or  for  the  entire  year.  If  the  government  emerging  from  the  elections  were  to  resume  fiscal  
consolidation only in 2028 (with a deficit of 5% of GDP that it projects to reduce to 3% by 2032), public debt would 
reach 126% of GDP by that date (a one-year delay in fiscal consolidation would result in an additional 2 pp of 
debt). This further delay could potentially undermine the credibility of the next government, given that budget 
targets were  not met in three out of four years between 2023 and 2026, a situation that would be costly amid a 
sharp rise in long-term interest rates. Furthermore, a higher deficit would increase the amount of debt to be issued 
within an already  tight schedule. The Agence France Trésor (AFT) has announced debt issuances totaling EUR 
340 billion in 2027 (an  increase  of  EUR  20  billion  compared  to  2026).  This  increase  is  due  to  the  maturing  
COVID  related  debt (beginning in 2027 and continuing largely through 2030), while the amount of debt to be 
issued to finance the deficit will remain stable compared to 2026. A decrease from this last driver would be needed 
in subsequent years so that  the AFT’s debt issuance can stabilise.

Stéphane Colliac 
Head of the Advanced Economies Team, France 
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

## Document 313: OpenAI员工长文：AI 时代的文明岔路

Source PDF: all report pdf/OpenAI员工长文：AI 时代的文明岔路.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-04
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

OpenAI 平台最新⽂章指出，前沿想法与落地执⾏⾼度互补。在超级智能时代，⽂明瓶颈将
从 “ 想不出 ” 变为 “ 做不完 ” 。未来或⾛向靠推理减少现实实验的 “ 深度⽂明 ” ，或需⽆限扩张基
础设施的 “ ⼴度⽂明 ” 。即便如此，⼈类的好奇⼼与创造⼒依然不可替代。
摘要：
美国当地时间 10 ⽉ 1 ⽇， OpenAI“ 智能时代 ” 平台发布新⽂章《 The Eternal Complement 》，作者
为赫曼斯 · 阿西尔⽡坦（ Hemanth Asirvatham ）和埃利奥特 · 莫克西（ Elliott Mokski ）。
这是两⼈ “ 下⼀个经济 ” 系列的第⼀篇⽂章，⽂章讨论的核⼼问题是：当 AI 的推理、创造和发现能
⼒不断增强，甚⾄⾛向超级智能之后，⽂明真正稀缺的资源会变成什么？
两位作者并没有把超级智能简单理解成更多 “ 天才 ” 的集合，⽽是从经济学中的 “ 互补 ” 概念出发，
重新讨论智能与现实执⾏能⼒之间的关系。⽂章认为，⼀个想法从诞⽣⾛向现实，需要⼤量配

套条件，包括实验设备、技术⼈员、资⾦、法律、官僚体系、供应链以及各种组织协调能⼒。
随着科学和技术不断向前推进，这些⽀撑体系本⾝也越来越庞⼤。
⽂章将这些⽀撑能⼒概括为 “ 制度智能 ” ，并认为前沿智能与执⾏能⼒始终是⼀组互补投⼊。在他
们看来，天才负责提出新的想法，制度和⼤量具体⼯作负责把想法变成现实。 AI 正在改变其中⼀
部分关系：它已经能够写代码、检索陌⽣领域的⽂献、把草图转化为原型，让个⼈完成过去需
要整个组织才能完成的⼯作。随着执⾏成本下降，真正稀缺的东西可能开始转向 “ 品味 ” ，也就是
判断什么值得做、什么问题值得追问。
不过， AI 带来的执⾏能⼒提升可能只是第⼀阶段。当 AI 本⾝开始⼤量产⽣新的科学洞⻅和研究⽅
向，问题可能发⽣反转：想法产⽣的速度超过实验室、⼯⼚、能源系统和其他现实基础设施的
承载能⼒。届时，⽂明⾯对的瓶颈可能再次从 “ 想不出来 ” 变成 “ 做不完 ” 。
围绕这⼀问题，⽂章提出了 “ 深度⽂明 ” 和 “ ⼴度⽂明 ” 两种可能的未来。前者依靠更强的推理、模

拟和已有证据，让智能在真正接触现实之前⾛得更远，⽤更少的实验获得更多知识；后者则受
到⾃然复杂性和物理世界速度的限制，⼤量问题最终仍然需要实验、建设和⻓期等待，⽂明需
要不断扩⼤实验设施、制造能⼒、能源系统和组织协调体系。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

⽂章最终将问题落回⼈类：即使 AI 未来在知识发现和资源协调⽅⾯超过⼈类，⼈类好奇⼼、创造
性多样性以及对探索的持续需求仍可能具有价值。他们给出三个理由：⼈类对启蒙和求知仍有
持续的责任；⼈类在前沿智能与制度智能之间可能存在不同于机器的⽐较优势；不同的⼈类个
体和群体能够带来机器难以替代的创造性多样性。
“ 智能时代 ” 平台于 2026 年 8 ⽉ 20 ⽇上线，由 OpenAI“ 战略未来 ” 团队成员迪恩 · 鲍尔（ Dean Ball ）
发布介绍⽂章。平台明确说明，所发布内容代表作者个⼈观点，不代表 OpenAI 的组织⽴场。
以下为全⽂。
01
进步需要⼀整套⽀撑系统
⼈类的认知已经追溯到了宇宙诞⽣的那⼀刻。我们的⽅程⼤致还原了宇宙最初的完整图景。借
助望远镜，我们已经望向遥远的宇宙，看到它早期的历史，知道恒星如何诞⽣、星系如何形
成。但迄今为⽌，还没有⼀个⼈⾛出过⽉球之外。
我们试图寻找的答案横跨整个宇宙，⽽⼈类的⾝体却⼏乎没有离开过地球。我们的思想可以抵
达远⽅，⼿脚却远远跟不上。这种错位指向两种完全不同的可能性。

⼀种可能是，我们其实不需要⾛那么远。最深层的真相，或许在⼀颗星球上就能探明。只要拥
有⾜够好的推理能⼒和⾼效的⼯具，我们就能认识宇宙，⽽不必亲⾃穿越宇宙。在这种情况
下，⽂明的发展依靠的是 “ 深度 ” ，⽽不是 “ ⼴度 ” 。这也可以解释为什么我们⾄今没有发现其他智
慧⽣命：也许银河系中存在许多拥有⾼超智慧的⽂明，只是它们从来没有必要不断向外扩张，
因此也没有留下明显的痕迹。
另⼀种可能则更令⼈不安：我们的思想可能已经超过了执⾏能⼒。每向前迈⼀步，都需要更多
时间和资源。我们从来不缺少梦想，缺少的是把这些梦想变成现实的⼈⼿。
伽利略⽤两⽚透镜和⼀根管⼦就拓展了⼈类的视野。今天，要把这种视野进⼀步向外延伸，⼈
类建造了詹姆斯 · ⻙布空间望远镜。这座价值 100 亿美元的天⽂台被折叠起来装⼊⽕箭，送到距
离地球约 160 万公⾥的太空。它的 18 块巨⼤镜⽚需要达到 50 纳⽶的制造精度，整个项⽬涉及来⾃
14 个国家的 300 多个组织。伽利略的⼯作只需要⼏⼗双⼿，⽽⻙布望远镜背后需要⼀⽀庞⼤的团

队，以及⽀撑这⽀团队运转的全球经济体系。
⼈类想看得更远，需要的不只是更聪明的头脑，也需要更庞⼤的组织。
我们提出的问题已经超过了能够实际研究的问题数量，设计出的实验也超过了能够真正执⾏的
实验数量。我们产⽣创意的速度越来越快，⽽把这些创意变成现实的能⼒却越来越难以跟上。
随着各个领域不断向前发展，制造我们想象中的东西也变得越来越复杂。
斯坦福⼤学的尼克 · 布鲁姆（ Nick Bloom ）及其合作者曾研究美国经济中的科研⽣产率。他们发
现，如今要维持摩尔定律，所需要的研究⼈员数量已经是 20 世纪 70 年代初的 18 倍以上。从整个
经济体来看，⾃ 20 世纪 30 年代以来，有效科研投⼊增加了 23 倍，但衡量科研⽣产率却下降了 41
倍。
技术⼈员的数量增⻓速度是科学家的两倍。过去 40 年⾥，科学研究中专业设备的使⽤量翻了⼀
倍。⼀座如今的芯⽚制造⼚，其成本已经是 30 年前的 5 倍，规模也⼤得多。

### Page 3

这些数据说明，继续推动进步的成本正在上升。经济仍然能够发展，是因为科研体系不断扩
⼤，⽤更多的研究投⼊抵消了单个投⼊的产出下降。
这也提醒我们，理解 “ 天才 ” 时，不能只看天才本⾝。⼀个出⾊的假设仍然需要证据、仪器以及把
它执⾏出来的条件。⼀个想法只有进⼊现实，才能真正成为进步。
经济学家把这种关系称为 “ 互补 ” ：当⼀种投⼊增加时，另⼀种投⼊的价值也会随之提⾼。前沿智
能和把想法变成现实的能⼒，就是这样的⼀对互补因素。更好的望远镜会让⼀个好的天⽂学问
题更有价值，⽽⼀个更好的问题也会让望远镜更加有⽤。
有些互补关系是物理层⾯的。⼀个理论需要粒⼦加速器来验证，⼀项技术设计需要能源和机器
才能制造出来。另⼀些互补关系则没有那么明显。⼀个想法本⾝⾮常脆弱，需要各种制度来⽀
撑。法律、官僚体系、融资机制、供应链以及其他环节，都需要共同运转，才能让⼀个想法真
正落地。
⼀个伟⼤的想法必须经过⼀条很⻓的链条，⽽链条上的每个局部环节都需要正确运⾏。我们可
以把这种能⼒称为 “ 制度智能 ” （ institutional intelligence ），也就是那些很少受到关注，却负责执

⾏的智能。天才设计出纪念碑，制度则负责⼀块块把⽯头垒起来。两者看起来差别很⼤，却同
样重要。
我们有时会把超级智能想象成 10 亿个爱因斯坦。但即使真的有 10 亿个爱因斯坦，他们中的⼤多
数仍然需要有⼈去采⽯、管理账⽬、维护设备。前沿创新只有在周围的⽇常世界能够顺畅运⾏
时才可能发⽣。从拧紧望远镜螺丝的焊⼯，到为培养护⼠的⼩学提供保险的⼈，所有这些⼯作
都构成了进步的⼀部分。
⼈们在讲述伟⼤发明时，往往只记住最耀眼的部分，却容易忽略⽀撑这些成果运转的经济体
系。
⼈⼯智能也会承担这两类⼯作：提出重要的新洞⻅，以及完成⼤量制度和执⾏层⾯的⼯作。真
正的问题在于，未来的 AI ⼤部分时间究竟会花在哪⾥。
02
新的瓶颈正在出现
AI 已经开始让执⾏能⼒变得不那么稀缺。它可以写代码、搜索陌⽣领域的⽂献，也可以把⼀张草
图变成能够运⾏的原型。过去需要整个组织才能推进的想法，现在越来越可能由⼀个有能⼒的

### Page 4

⼈独⽴完成。
⼤多数有抱负的电影导演，从来没有机会获得 2 亿美元去拍摄⾃⼰的作品 . 很多游戏设计师整个职
业⽣涯都在替别⼈实现创意。我们会努⼒把最有天赋的⼈提拔出来，但⽆论如何，都难以发现
所有⼈才。
从这个⻆度来看，智能时代最初带来的变化，是让更多⼈的创造⼒得到⽀持，让更多⼈拥有过
去只有⼤型机构才能提供的执⾏能⼒。
这可能带来⼤量具有个⼈特⾊的项⽬。过去需要依靠机构才能完成的⼯作，未来可能更多地发
⽣在这些机构之外。这样⼀来，稀缺的资源就会从执⾏转向 “ 品味 ”—— 判断什么值得做，什么问
题值得追问，以及⾯对上千条看似合理的⽅向时，究竟应该选择哪⼀条。
但这可能还不是故事的终点。随着 AI 越来越有能⼒⾃⼰提出新的重要洞⻅，我们可能已经在数学
等领域看到这种趋势。 AI 提供的将不只是劳动，还可能开始提供研究议程。它不再只是执⾏⼀个
⼈提出的研究计划，⽽是可以⾃⼰设计成千上万个研究计划。
AI 可能⼀下⼦提出⼤量值得拍摄的电影概念，也可能提出⼤量值得验证的科学假设。这样⼀来，

问题就会再次发⽣变化。今天的 AI 让我们终于能够把许多好想法付诸实践。到了未来，也许会出
现太多好想法，以⾄于我们再次陷⼊执⾏能⼒不⾜的困境。
03
两种⽂明的岔路⼝
我们并不知道，把新的洞⻅变成成果所需要的互补能⼒，未来究竟会增加、减少，还是保持不
变。⼀个⼈仅靠思考可以在多⼤程度上推动知识进步？⼀个天才究竟需要多少官僚体系来配
合？
在我们看来，答案取决于思想在必须再次接触现实之前，能够⾛多远。
这⾥可以设想两条不同的路径。
第⼀条是 “ 深度⽂明 ” （ civilization of depth ）。在这种⽂明中，超级智能可能逐渐摆脱对更多物质
资本和复杂制度协调的需求。
第⼆条是 “ 宽度⽂明 ” （ civilization of width ）。在这种⽂明中，⾃然界的复杂程度最终超过任何智
能，⽆论是⼈类还是机器，都⽆法只靠思考继续取得进展，必须依靠规模越来越⼤的现实世界
实验。

### Page 5

到⽬前为⽌，⼈类历史似乎显⽰出对互补能⼒不断增⻓的需求。伽利略的望远镜可以握在⼿
中，⽽⻙布望远镜需要整个⽂明来建造。⻉尔实验室（ Bell Labs ）⼀个规模不⼤的团队，就曾
在⼯作台上组装出第⼀只晶体管 . 如今，芯⽚技术的前沿突破背后却是⼀条复杂的全球供应链。
⼈类每向前⾛⼀步，都越来越需要更多社会资源参与其中。
但过去并不⼀定决定未来。更聪明的头脑可以发明更⾼效的互补条件。如果能源成为新型粒⼦
加速器的瓶颈，那么更强的智能或许能够设计更精简的加速器，或者找到更好的能源获取⽅
式。
天才也能够替代部分原本需要的辅助条件。爱因斯坦可以从⾮常有限的实验数据出发推导出相
对论，⽽我们⼤多数⼈需要更多资料。有些⼈能够记住⾃⼰的所有⽇程安排，⽽⼤多数⼈依靠
⾕歌⽇历。
智能还可以帮助我们提⾼选择的准确性，挑选最值得验证的假设，避免把资源浪费在错误的⽅
向上。如果每增加⼀个天才，都能提⾼计划的质量，那么即使执⾏所需要的官僚体系越来越庞
⼤，增加天才的价值仍然很⾼，因为执⾏成本越⾼，错误选择所造成的浪费就越⼤。

未来不会完全符合这两条路径中的任何⼀条，但它们可以看作⽂明可能发展的两个⽅向。
04
深度⽂明：靠思想⾛得更远
智能推动知识进步，⼤致有三种⽅式：从基本原理出发进⾏推理，从已有证据中获得新的洞
⻅，以及通过观察和⼲预现实世界获取新的证据。
第⼀种⽅式可以仅靠思想⾛得很远。数学就是最典型的例⼦。在⼀项证明或者研究⼯作的多个
关键节点上，⼀个⾜够强⼤的头脑可以持续做出正确选择，把原本需要多年摸索的过程变成⼀
条更短、更明确的推理链。
第⼆种⽅式同样存在巨⼤的空间。⼈类收集到的证据远远超过已经理解的内容。天⽂图像、显
微镜切⽚、⽹络评论等⼤量资料，都受到⼈类智能有限的制约。科学档案中保存着⼤量当年为
了回答某⼀个问题⽽记录的数据，⽽这些数据可能包含着其他问题的答案。如果拥有⾜够多的
智能，我们甚⾄不需要收集新的数据，就可能从已有资料中发现新的规律。我们也许可以发现
⼀个新的宇宙，⽽不需要再收集⼀个新的光⼦。

### Page 6

第三种⽅式就困难得多，因为它需要与物理世界直接接触。物理过程需要时间，⽽且很多时候
⽆法加速。化学反应需要发⽣，⽣物体需要⽣⻓，机器需要制造，航天器需要穿越真实的距
离，并受到宇宙速度极限的限制。即使拥有完美的智能，也⽆法提前观察⼀个尚未发⽣的实验
结果。
但超级智能可以尽量减少与现实世界接触的次数。 “ 深度⽂明 ” 并不会摆脱对现实的依赖，⽽是会
尽可能节省这种依赖。更好的推理可以找出真正值得进⾏的少数实验，模拟则可以解决⼤部分
问题，并准确找到必须由现实世界回答的那个不确定性。超级智能可以围绕有限资源进⾏设
计，让每⼀次现实实验都获得最⼤的价值。
例如，⼀个研究暗物质的天体物理实验室，可能⾸先把⼀系列假设交给⾼度逼真的计算机模型
进⾏测试，检查它们是否与现有数据⼀致。只有完成这些步骤后，研究⼈员才会针对最有希望
的理论，对天空进⾏少量新的观测。
这种⽂明仍然需要⽀撑天才的各种条件，但每⼀单位资源能够产⽣的价值会更⾼。
“ 深度⽂明 ” 也可能像⼀幅拼图。进步在开始阶段⽐较容易，接近完成时也可能变得容易，最困难

的是中间阶段。刚开始时，我们能找到明显的边缘和简单的拼接⽅式，等这些部分逐渐完成，
剩下的图案还不够完整，却已经没有那么多容易确定的位置。到了最后，剩余的空缺反⽽会逐
渐告诉我们应该填⼊什么。
科学史上已经有类似的例⼦。⻔捷列夫可以根据周期表中已有元素的位置，推测当时尚未发现
的元素。标准模型也让物理学家在希格斯玻⾊⼦真正被观测到⼏⼗年前，就已经有理由预期它
的存在。很多时候，实验本⾝就是针对现有理论和数据所指向的结果设计的。
如果超级智能能够⾜够深⼊地理解⾃然界的规律，它也许能够准确判断下⼀步究竟需要什么数
据。⽂明的进步因此可以更多依靠深⼊思考，⽽不必不断扩⼤现实世界中的实验规模。

### Page 7

如果这种效率最终占据主导，⽂明或许能够以较⼩的物理规模不断加深对⾃然的理解，同时减
少管理庞⼤实验设施所需要的制度智能。
⼈类已经在不离开地球的情况下理解了⼤量宇宙规律。 “ 深度⽂明 ” 只是沿着这条道路继续前进。
05
⼴度⽂明：当思想跑不过物质
另⼀种可能是，对新证据的需求最终超过这些效率提升所带来的收益。
智能可以降低每⼀次接触现实的成本，但同时也可能让我们发现更多必须通过现实验证的问
题。更好的仪器可以打开更细的研究前沿，更快的实验也会让更多实验变得值得进⾏。如果爱
因斯坦⼏乎完全依靠思考建⽴相对论的过程，可以代表 “ 深度⽂明 ” ，那么⽣物学可能更接近 “ ⼴
度⽂明 ” 。
⼈类已经积累了⼤量⽣物学知识和数据，但⼀种新药要证明安全、有效，仍然需要在⼤量⼈类
⾝上进⾏测试。如今，机器可以模拟⽣物过程，让研究⼈员先在计算机中测试候选药物，但这
些数字模拟仍然不⾜以完全还原现实，因此⽆法替代⼤规模⼈体试验。
机器提出的候选⽅案越多，现实验证就越容易成为瓶颈。在 “ ⼴度⽂明 ” 中，聪明的⼤脑本⾝不⾜

以推动进步。对物质资源的需求，以及管理这些资源所需要的制度体系，可能以更快的速度增
⻓，超过智能节约资源的能⼒。
即使未来的 AI 远远超过⼈类，这种情况也可能发⽣。超级智能或许可以在⼀个下午设计出未来
100 年的实验，然后花 100 年等待⾃然和机器完成这些实验。在这样的世界⾥，把更多智能投⼊
实验、⽣产和制度执⾏，可能⽐继续增加提出新想法的能⼒更加重要。
如果每⼀步新的进展都需要更多基础设施，这种趋势还会不断累积。那时，⽂明中的思考会相
对减少，建造会越来越多。实验室实现⾃动化，⼯⼚不断增加，能源⽣产扩⼤，越来越多的制
度智能被投⼊到这些体系中，以保证它们能够正常运⾏。
物理世界的扩张会成为知识进步的动⼒。⽂明之所以继续向外扩展，是因为它提出的问题已经
超过了现有条件能够回答的范围，需要更多材料、更多能源和更多数据才能继续前进。

### Page 8

戴森球可以作为这种未来的极端例⼦。建造戴森球需要⾮常重要的科学突破，但整个项⽬的⼤
部分⼯作可能不会发⽣在突破性思考上，⽽是涉及天⽂尺度的建造和物流。它需要天才来设
计，也需要⼀个规模巨⼤、重复⽽复杂的组织体系来完成建设。
这并⾮智能不⾜造成的结果，反⽽可能是智能取得成功后的结果。越聪明的头脑能够发现越多
值得开展的项⽬，⽽越复杂的项⽬⼜需要更多物质资源来执⾏。
如果未来确实如此，那么超级智能真正强⼤的地⽅可能并不是它有多么聪明，⽽是它能够承担
⼤量协调⼯作，管理庞⼤的技术和制度体系，把想法变成现实，并不断扩⼤规模。绝⼤多数机
器智能最终可能都被⽤于那些 “ ⽆聊 ” 的⼯作，⽽不是最具创造性的⼯作。
物理世界本⾝的速度限制，也可能成为进⼀步扩张的瓶颈。建造⼀套收集恒星能源的机器，本
质上仍然是⼀个物质⼯程，⽆论设计⽅案能够多快完成，真正的建设都需要时间。恒星之外还
有其他恒星，银河系之外还有其他星系。就⽬前所知，距离本⾝就是⼀种限制：没有⻜船能够
超过光速，也没有信号能够以超过光速的速度返回。

因此， “ ⼴度⽂明 ” 会是⼀个缓慢的⽂明。它真正发⽣的⼤变化可能相隔数千年，甚⾄数百万年，
每⼀步都需要更⻓时间。
农业⾰命和⼯业⾰命，在这样的历史尺度下，可能只是⼀个漫⻓⽂明史中相对短暂的开端。
06
⼈类仍然需要好奇⼼

### Page 9

未来的 AI 系统可能同时在⼈类最看重的两件事情上超过我们：提出前沿洞⻅，以及协调资源，把
这些洞⻅真正转化为现实成果。但这并不会降低⼈类好奇⼼的价值。
⾸先，即使机器在理解世界⽅⾯⽐我们更强，⼈类仍然有继续认识世界的责任。宇宙中可能已
经存在⽐我们智慧得多的头脑，它们或许掌握着⼈类⽆法理解的科学和道德问题的答案。但我
们依然会尝试⾃⼰理解这个世界。即使另⼀种智能出现了，即使这种智能是我们⾃⼰创造出来
的，也不会改变这⼀点。
在我们能够确定⼈类已经没有任何独特贡献之前，放弃探索都意味着放弃我们仍然能够为这个
世界做出的事情。我们仍然有责任不断提出问题，并尝试寻找答案。
其次，⼈类可能在前沿智能⽅⾯拥有相对于制度智能的⽐较优势。即使机器在这两⽅⾯都⽐⼈
类更强，我们也可能相对更擅⻓前者，因此可以把⾃⼰的精⼒集中在创造和探索上。⼈类倾向
于选择创造性的⼯作，⽽不是重复性的⼯作。机器⼈则没有这样的偏好。
第三，多样性本⾝也有价值。即使机器能够模拟⼤部分⼈类思维，只要⼈类仍然保留⼀些独特
性，就能够继续提供不同的创造⽅式。

未来，⼤部分机器智能可能运⾏在⾃⼰的体系中，但其中⼀⼩部分仍然可以按照⼈类的创造意
愿被调⽤。对于整个机器智能体系来说，这只是⼀⼩部分；但对于⼈类⽽⾔，这已经可能远远
超过我们今天能够获得的资源。
创造⼒从来都不是孤⽴存在的。它需要现实世界作为⽀撑，天才也需要建造者来实现⾃⼰的想
法。今天，⼈类的⼤量智能都被⽤于那些很少受到关注的⼯作：把想法⼀步步变成现实。未来

中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

⼈类未来的进步速度，最终取决于⼀个问题：伟⼤的头脑究竟能否⽤更少的现实资源，完成更

## Document 314: OpenAI宣布GPT-6提速50%，额度却被锤只有Claude的1_5

Source PDF: all report pdf/OpenAI宣布GPT-6提速50%，额度却被锤只有Claude的1_5.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

为什么要收紧订阅？ SemiAnalysis 算了⼀笔账。订阅是⾼度补贴的产品，以  Anthropic 为
例，据估算，订阅只占总收⼊的约  10% ，却可能吃掉  40% 以上的推理算⼒，让每兆⽡的
混合收⼊减少约  3600 万美元。对订阅收⼊占⽐更⾼的  OpenAI 来说，这个问题只会更突
出。
摘要：
昨天， Tibo 承诺「连续  28 天发布或重置」。刚刚，⾸个更新来了。
Tibo 宣布， OpenAI 已优化默认速度：订阅⽤⼾在所有产品中使⽤  GPT-6 Astra 和  GPT-6.1
Sol ，速度提升约  50% 。⽤⼾⽆需做任何操作，两⼩时内就能感受到变化。
同时， OpenAI 官⽅还宣布了⽂本⽔印功能：
⽽就在⼏乎同⼀时间， SemiAnalysis 发布了⼀份订阅实测报告，标题毫不客⽓：Anthropic 订
阅提供的价值是  OpenAI 的  5 倍以上。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

OpenAI 可能也没想到，⾃⼰刚兑现承诺就被  SemiAnalysis 这家著名第三⽅机构正⾯打脸。不
仅如此，就连  OpenAI 这次更新本⾝，也被很多⽹友吐槽⼒度不够，不如⼀次重置。
昨天我们写到，开发者⽇之后， OpenAI 正⾯临⼀场⽤⼾信任危机，重度⽤⼾的不满集中在两件
事上：模型慢，额度少。第  1 天回应了前者， SemiAnalysis 的报告则把后者摆上了台⾯。
第  1 天：先解决「慢」
Tibo 随后在回复中补充了数字：输出速度从  30 TPS 提升到  50 TPS ，并搭配了他所说的⽬前优
化程度最⾼的  tokenizer 。他还特别强调，这两个模型完成任务所需的  token 数量本⾝就很少。
这次提速不只覆盖  OpenAI ⾃家产品，也包括所有通过「 Sign in with ChatGPT 」接⼊的合作伙
伴， Tibo 点名了  OpenCode 、 Pi 、 Amp 、 Devin 等。换句话说，只要是⽤  ChatGPT 订阅跑这
两个模型，⽆论在哪⾥调⽤，都会变快。

但有开发者在评论区表⽰，即便如此， ChatGPT 模型依然⽐  Claude 慢。

### Page 3

还有⼀些开发者则在评论区表⽰了质疑，并有⼀些实测结果晒出，⽐如  @BrenBuilds 晒出的实
测数据显⽰， GPT-6.1 的速度不过是回到了该模型发布当前的速度。
同样花钱，差了  5 倍
但速度不等于额度。第  1 天的更新没有提到任何使⽤限额的变化，⽽这正是  SemiAnalysis 报告
的主⻆。
这份报告对  Anthropic 、 OpenAI 、 Meta 、 SpaceXAI 、 MiniMax 、⽉之暗⾯（ Moonshot ）、智谱
（ Z.ai ）、 Cursor 和  Cognition 的订阅⽅案逐⼀做了极限测试。
订阅⽅案不会公开细粒度的价格，⽤⼾只能看到⼀个  0 到  100% 的⽤量进度条，通常分为  5 ⼩
时和  7 天两个窗⼝。

### Page 4

SemiAnalysis 的做法是把输⼊、缓存写⼊、缓存读取、输出四类  token 逐⼀隔离，⽤专⻔设计
的  prompt 反复调⽤，记录进度条每跳⼀格消耗了多少  token ，直到误差收敛到  ±5% 以内。最
后再按  API 价格折算，得到每份订阅的「 API 等价价值」，也就是这份额度如果按  API 计费值多
少钱。
SemiAnalysis 发现，同⼀家⼚商的三个相同订阅⾥，有⼀个账号的额度⽐另外两个低约  20% 。
与⼚商沟通后才确认，这个账号被分进了⼀个规模极⼩的额度  A/B 测试。这也说明，⼚商可以
随时、静默地调整订阅额度，普通⽤⼾⼏乎⽆从察觉。
先看旗舰档。 GPT-6 Astra 和  Claude Fable 5.1 的额度其实相当接近，区别在于  Fable 在
Claude 订阅中最多只能占⽤总额度的⼀半。在  200 美元档位上，⽤掉价值  2485 美元的  Fable
5.1 之后， Claude 订阅还剩⼀半额度可以留给其他模型；同档  OpenAI 订阅在消耗完价值  2897

美元的  Astra 后，就彻底⻅底了。
真正拉开差距的是中档模型，也就是两家都定位为⼤多数⽤⼾「⽇常主⼒」的  Claude Opus 5.5
和  GPT-6.1 Sol 。在这⼀层级， Anthropic 订阅的  API 等价价值约为  OpenAI 的  5 倍，并且在各

### Page 5

个价位档全⾯领先。
有⼈会说，这样⽐对  OpenAI 不公平： 6.1 Sol 的  API 单价本就⽐  Opus 5.5 便宜得多，按  API
价格折算⾃然吃亏。 SemiAnalysis 预判了这个质疑，即便改为直接⽐较  token 数量，差距依然
很⼤。
OpenAI 能拿出来的唯⼀反驳，是它的  Pro 套餐没有  5 ⼩时限制，⽤⼾更容易把⽉度额度真正
⽤满。但  SemiAnalysis 认为，这不⾜以抵消  Opus 5.5 在  Claude 订阅上约  4 倍的价值优势。
报告判断：独⽴开发者过去常常称赞  OpenAI 的订阅额度⽐  Anthropic 慷慨，这个说法如今已经
不成⽴了。
额度砍半， 500 美元档只多  21%
差距之所以这么⼤，很⼤程度上来⾃  OpenAI 上周的那次调整，参阅《 OpenAI 将重新开放  200
美元套餐，但  Tibo 被骂惨了》。
SemiAnalysis 的追踪数据证实了  Tibo 此前宣布的变化： OpenAI 200 美元套餐的  API 等价价值

被砍掉⼀半。具体来说，每个模型档位可⽤的  token 数量都减半；⼜因为  GPT-6.1 Sol 同时下
调了缓存输⼊价格， Sol 级模型的  API 等价价值跌幅超过  50% 。降额前购买的  200 美元套餐可
以保留旧额度到  10 ⽉  29 ⽇，新购⽤⼾则⽴即适⽤新额度。

### Page 6

同期推出的  500 美元新档位，Astra 额度只⽐旧版  200 美元套餐多  21% ，Sol 级模型的  API 等
价价值甚⾄不升反降。这⼀档真正的卖点是  300 TPS 的  Ultrafast 模式， SemiAnalysis 表⽰其
额度仍在测试中。
在此之前， 200 美元档是  OpenAI 补贴⼒度最⼤的套餐。按每美元价值计算， Pro 100 在  Astra
上约为  Plus 的  2 倍， Pro 200 ⼜在  Pro 100 的基础上再翻⼀倍。调整之后， Pro 100 、 200 和
500 三档每美元能换到的  token 完全⼀致，「买⼤档更划算」的逻辑不复存在。
很多⽤⼾的抱怨「付的钱没少，⽤量变少了」，现在有了第三⽅数据作注脚。当然，这个第三⽅
数据本⾝也受到了⼀些质疑：

### Page 7

两种降补贴思路
为什么要收紧订阅？ SemiAnalysis 算了⼀笔账。订阅是⾼度补贴的产品，以  Anthropic 为例，
据估算，订阅只占总收⼊的约  10% ，却可能吃掉  40% 以上的推理算⼒，让每兆⽡的混合收⼊减
少约  3600 万美元。
对订阅收⼊占⽐更⾼的  OpenAI 来说，这个问题只会更突出。
两家都想降低补贴，路径却截然不同。
Anthropic ⾛的是渐进路线：越⾼端的模型，订阅⾥给的  API 等价价值越低。 Sonnet 5.5 与
Opus 5.5 之间差距不⼤，到了  Fable 5.1 则明显收缩。 SemiAnalysis 测算，在  100% 使⽤率、
API ⽑利率  92% 的假设下，⽤满  Opus 5.5 对应的订阅⽑利率是  -369% ，⽤满  Fable 5.1 约为
1% ；如果按更现实的  20% 平均使⽤率计算，两者分别为  6% 和  80% 。换句话说，如果所有⼈
只⽤  Fable ， Anthropic 的订阅业务很可能已经拥有软件公司级别的利润率。

### Page 8

OpenAI 选的则是被  SemiAnalysis 称为「核选项（ nuclear option ）」的做法：直接把所有模型
的额度⼀⼑切，降到  Fable 级别。
按理说，这种做法应该招致强烈反弹。但  SemiAnalysis 认为， OpenAI 在很⼤程度上躲过了这
⼀劫，可能的原因是开发者⽇的⼤量正⾯宣传冲淡了前⼀天的降额消息，加上⽼⽤⼾还能多保
留⼀个⽉旧额度。
只是从这⼏天社交媒体上的情绪来看，这次「躲过」也许只是推迟。
剩下的  27 天
第  1 天的提速是实打实的改进。 Tibo 也给出了  OpenAI ⾃⼰的辩护逻辑：模型完成任务需要的
token 更少，单⽐  token 数量或  API 等价价值，并不能完全反映实际产出。这个论点有⼀定道
理， SemiAnalysis ⾃⼰也承认，当某个模型按  API 价格看特别贵或特别便宜时， API 等价价值
会失真。
但「每个任务能省多少  token 」⽬前还没有第三⽅的量化数据，⽽额度减半已经是被测出来的事
实。

还有⼀个时间点需要留意：降额前购买的  200 美元套餐，旧额度将在  10 ⽉  29 ⽇到期，正好落
在这  28 天的窗⼝期内。到那时，此前被「保护」起来的⼀批重度⽤⼾，也会直接感受到额度的
变化。

### Page 9

⻛险提⽰及免责条款

中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

## Document 343: The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

The end of the safety net:  
Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

28 September 2026 
For several decades, financial markets and the wider economy benefited from an implicit 
safety net. If a slowdown or a downturn arrived, there was a monetary put from central 
banks, and a fiscal put from governments. The monetary put would see interest rates 
slashed and liquidity injected via QE. The fiscal put would see governments run larger 
deficits to cushion any downturn. These policy stabilisers smoothed out the business 
cycle, and supported asset valuations too. 
These policy interventions were increasingly normalised from the late-1980s, and became 
more aggressive with each passing cycle. This reached its peak during the Covid-19 
pandemic, when governments ran fiscal deficits unprecedented in peacetime, whilst 
many central banks expanded their balance sheets with huge asset purchases. 
But after an era of ever-bigger interventions, these old stabilisers now face major 
obstacles. On the fiscal side, policy is constrained today by sovereign yields at multi-year 
highs, alongside the highest debt-to-GDP ratios in several decades. Then on the monetary 
side, policy is constrained by persistent inflation that’s increasingly supply-driven. 
This represents a big shift from the last few decades, but it does echo previous regimes 
where the macroeconomic stabilisers were either blunted or left unused. A good example 
is the 1970s experience, when stagflation and supply shocks posed a dilemma to 
traditional policy tools. Even further back, the pre-WWI environment of the classical gold 
standard and balanced budget orthodoxy saw governments play a very different and more 
limited role in macroeconomic stabilisation. 
Looking forward, this environment means policymakers will have a much more difficult 
time stabilising the business cycle in the coming years. So absent a sharp fall in yields or a 
collapse in inflation, higher macro volatility will be here to stay.

### Page 2

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
1. Background: How the stabilisers used to work.
Economic policymakers have long used interventions to stabilise output. This
became increasingly fashionable after WWII, when Keynesian economics
established a more active use for fiscal policy to stimulate the economy in a
downturn. This meant budget deficits became a more regular feature of life, rather
than used solely for wartime emergencies.

It was from the late 1980s, however, that the extent of policy interventions became
increasingly sizeable. Indeed, it was here that the origins of a central bank “put” for
financial markets began, in the crisis of Black Monday in 1987. That was a huge
stock market crash, where the S&P 500 fell over 20% in a single day, marking the
biggest daily decline in its entire history. The size of the falls even went beyond the
Great Depression, raising fears of wider contagion, particularly if it were to spark a

liquidity crisis that caused financial institutions to fail. However, the Federal
Reserve responded by cutting rates and providing liquidity, offering a
macroeconomic stabiliser against the prospect of wider economic stress. This
offered support against a wider downturn, and the US economic expansion
continued until 1990.
Whilst the genesis of a central bank “put” came in 1987, the idea gained further
traction in the late-1990s, with the Asian financial crisis in 1997, followed by the

Russian financial crisis in 1998. Fears of an economic contraction mounted,
particularly after issues at Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) raised concerns
that the US wasn’t immune from these global pressures. So once again, the Fed
delivered three 25bp rate cuts pre-emptively, seeking to support the economy.
A few years later, the bursting of the dot com bubble in early 2000 set the stage for
yet further stimulus. As equity markets declined, the Fed began to cut rates from

early 2001, ultimately delivering 475bps of cuts that year that took rates from 6.5%
to just 1.75%. That was followed up by further rate cuts in 2002 and 2003, leaving
rates at 1% by mid-2003 into 2004. Moreover, the Bush tax cuts were also delivered
in 2001 and 2003, acting as a fiscal stimulus as the wider US economy entered
recession.
In hindsight, these moves seem modest compared to what then happened in 2008,
when the Global Financial Crisis led to even bigger policy interventions. Suddenly,

the world faced the biggest economic contraction since the Great Depression, with
genuine fears that a repeat might happen. To avoid this, policymakers launched into
the biggest economic stimulus ever seen in a peacetime environment. That
included massive fiscal stimulus, with the US federal deficit at 9.8% of GDP in 2008,
the highest since 1945. Moreover, central banks slashed rates to the zero lower
bound, and many like the Federal Reserve launched into Quantitative Easing

programmes, purchasing assets to ease financial conditions even more. Over the
coming years, many began pledging to keep rates low well into the future,
pioneering the concept of forward guidance.
These levels of policy intervention weren’t confined to the immediate crisis
however. In the subsequent years, active attempts were made to prolong and
extend the business cycle, particularly as the 2010s became an era of low inflation
and low interest rates. In this environment, borrowing was historically cheap, and

low inflation meant central banks could cut rates without seeing a big overshoot of
their targets. Indeed, both the Fed and the ECB undershot their 2% inflation target
on average in the 2010s, and the ECB took their own deposit rate into negative
territory in 2014.

### Page 3

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3

targets on the downside in the 2010s (PCE inflation for the US, HICP inflation for

By 2019, the year before the pandemic, the intellectual climate had continued to
move in the direction of more policy intervention. Government bond yields hit new

lows, with Germany’s 10-year bund yield at -0.7% in summer 2019. This
environment of negative yields meant borrowing was basically a “free lunch”, and
that summer also saw the Fed begin a 75bp easing cycle, as inflation was below
their target and they wanted to sustain the expansion.
The peak of this policy interventionism was seen with the Covid-19 pandemic in
2020. Just as in 2008, the world was facing a huge and sudden economic
contraction. Moreover, the consensus among policymakers was that they could

have done more to speed up the recovery after 2008, potentially avoiding years of
very high unemployment. As such, 2020 saw the most aggressive economic
stimulus packages ever seen in a peacetime environment, well above those in 2008.
Central banks immediately slashed rates back to the zero lower bound, pledged to
keep rates low for years to come, and launched into major QE programmes again.
Then on the fiscal side, record peacetime deficits were run. Indeed, the US federal

deficit of 14.5% of GDP in 2020 was the highest since 1945.

### Page 4

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
During the pandemic period, many initially expected these interventions to remain
for years to come. After all, in mid-2020, financial markets continued to price a
continuation of the low inflation and low interest rates of the 2010s. This belief was
clear on several fronts, and the 10-year Treasury yield closed at just 0.51% in August
2020. Meanwhile, the Federal Reserve even adjusted its own framework to allow for

average inflation targeting, i.e. permitting an overshoot of the inflation target after
periods of an undershoot. This reflected the consensus view that inflation was
unlikely to be a serious threat.
But as we now know, inflation returned in a serious way after the pandemic, forcing
central banks into rate hikes. Meanwhile, public debt-to-GDP ratios rose
significantly thanks to the pandemic stimulus, helping to push up sovereign bond
yields too. In other words, many of the usual policy tools of the previous few

decades were either blunted or no longer available.
2. The Monetary Constraint: High inflation means the central
bank put isn’t available
In the pre-2020 world described above, central banks regularly grappled with how
to generate enough inflation. In part, this was thanks to several structural forces
that put downward pressure on global prices. For instance, deepening
globalisation acted as a positive supply shock that kept costs down. Meanwhile, the

expansion of the global labour force in these years also boosted the supply side. In
other words, inflation was low and central banks generally had the space to cut
interest rates when they needed to.
But since the pandemic, we have entered a very different environment of above-
target inflation across many of the world’s biggest economies. Initially, that was
caused by a huge wave of pent-up demand after the pandemic, alongside
disruption to supply chains and labour markets. But the subsequent years also

brought further shocks. Those included Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in early 2022,
driving a big spike in European energy prices, and pushing Euro Area inflation above
10% briefly. In 2026, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz then acted as a fresh energy
shock, pushing inflation further away from target and contributing to a fresh
tightening cycle from the major central banks.
Figure 4: In the 2020s, inflation has generally been above central bank targets

(PCE inflation for the US, HICP inflation for the Euro Area)
-2%

Federal Reserve ECB
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank

### Page 5

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Alongside those shocks, the structural forces keeping inflation down have gone
into reverse. For instance, demographic trends have seen working-age populations
shrink in many places. Meanwhile, the trend towards deeper globalisation has
stalled, and the focus has turned to near-shoring and supply-chain resilience, rather
than locating production in the cheapest place. Simultaneously, other trends like

the push for higher defence spending are also adding to demand-side pressures.
Because of all this, the 2020s so far have brought persistently above-target inflation,
and central bankers have found it much more difficult to ease policy in response to
growth wobbles or financial stability fears.
This initially became apparent in 2022, amidst the biggest inflation wave for several
decades. Central banks had to keep hiking rates, even in the face of slowing growth

and financial market stress. Indeed, despite the S&P 500 being in bear market
territory by mid-2022, the Federal Reserve still launched into a series of 75bp hikes,
the biggest hikes since the 1990s. Then in 2023, despite the collapse of Silicon
Valley Bank and fears of wider financial contagion, the Fed and ECB still continued
with their rate hikes to combat inflation.
That tightening cycle in 2022-23 was a significant moment, because high inflation

meant monetary policy could not simply act as a stabiliser against growth or
financial stress. After all, policymakers realised that if they didn’t act to tackle high
inflation, they risked a repeat of the 1970s and the potential for even harsher
monetary tightening to get inflation under control again.
Today in 2026, this is a familiar problem again. We are in a globally synchronised
hiking cycle, with the Fed, ECB and Bank of Japan all having delivered rate hikes.

So far at least, global growth has been robust and financial conditions remain
accommodative by historical standards. But because inflation remains clearly
above target, central banks don’t have the space they did in the pre-pandemic world
to simply cut rates at the first sign of difficulty. There is still an inflation mandate to
be achieved, and given we have now seen multiple years of elevated inflation, there
is a much higher risk of inflation expectations becoming unanchored in that

context, particularly if another shock occurred.
3. The Fiscal Constraint: High yields and high public debt
mean fiscal stimulus isn’t easily available either
Just as monetary policy is constrained by high inflation, fiscal policy today is also
constrained by higher yields and higher public debt. This is a big transition from the
world of 1980-2020, when yields were generally falling throughout. Indeed, despite
all the fiscal stimulus around the GFC, the continuous decline in yields over the

2010s meant that debt dynamics remained manageable, because lower yields
helped to offset the impact of higher debt.
Since the pandemic however, we’ve been in a very different world, as the dual
forces of higher debt and higher yields have made debt dynamics far more
unfavourable around the world. And this isn’t just a nominal story, as real yields
have also risen to multi-year highs as well.

### Page 6

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
Figure 5: 10yr Government Bond Yields (%) have risen to multi-year highs across
several countries

In essence, there are 4 new constraints around fiscal stimulus.
First, is the significant rise in global yields. By definition, the cost of incurring debt
has grown, in both nominal and real terms. Relative to the pre-pandemic baseline

in early 2020, the 10-year US Treasury yield has risen from 1.9% to 5.2%. Similarly
in Germany, the 10-year bund yield has risen from -0.2% to 3.6%. Moreover, with
yields already elevated, any further borrowing risks crowding out the private sector,
and further dampening growth. And for governments, interest spending has
ramped up significantly. For instance, US net interest spending as a share of GDP
exceeded its recent peak from the early 1990s in the most recent fiscal year, and the

CBO forecast this is set to continue.
Figure 6: US Net Interest Costs (% of GDP) with CBO Forecasts
0
1

Source : CBO, Deutsche Bank
The second constraint is that public debt itself is now at a higher level. This interacts
with the higher yields mentioned above, because even though yields have only
returned to their levels from the mid-2000s, that is much more impactful because
they are being paid on a much larger debt pile. Using IMF numbers for consistency,

net public debt across the advanced economies has risen from 52% of GDP in 2000,
to 80% in 2025, with further increases projected to 2030. This means the fiscal
space available to governments is more limited than it used to be.

### Page 7

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
The third constraint on fiscal stimulus is the high starting level of budget deficits. So
to generate a meaningful fiscal stimulus, it would require deficits to be pushed up
to even higher levels than they are today. But that in turn would add to the risks of
bond market disruption that cascades through the economy, leading to higher
borrowing costs for corporates and consumers as well.
The fourth constraint is higher inflation. When inflation is high, any fiscal stimulus

that boosts demand risks pushing up prices rather than boosting output, and in turn
makes monetary tightening more likely that could offset the impact of the stimulus.
This trade-off was apparent in 2022, when central banks were hiking rates
aggressively, and even further back in the inflationary environment of the 1970s. In
a world of high inflation and supply constraints, fiscal stimulus is more likely to
overheat the economy.
4. Are there any other eras of history where the stabilisers

weren’t available?
Whilst history never quite repeats exactly, there have been other eras in which the
traditional policy stabilisers were either unavailable or severely blunted, which hold
some lessons for our own time.
The pre-WWI classical gold standard
Before WWI, the international monetary system was known as the gold standard,
in which currencies were convertible into a fixed quantity of gold. In practice
however, this acted as a huge constraint on macroeconomic stabilisation. After all,

monetary policy had to maintain the convertibility of gold at a fixed parity. This
meant contemporary options like QE weren’t available in a crisis. Meanwhile on the
fiscal side, governments generally adhered to balanced budget orthodoxy. This was
before Keynes wrote the General Theory, so the modern understanding of
countercyclical deficit spending was not in place. In the US, there wasn’t even a
central bank until the Federal Reserve’s establishment in 1913.

Figure 7: Gold Prices (US Dollars per ounce, log scale)
1
10

10000

parity
Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank

### Page 8

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Without modern policy stabilisers however, this was also an era of huge
macroeconomic volatility. There was a major financial panic across Europe and the
US in 1873, which marked the start of the so-called “Long Depression”. In the US,
that contraction lasted until 1879, which in length was even longer than the Great
Depression contraction of 1929-33. And this was followed by other major panics,

including the 1893 panic that led to another US contraction, along with the 1907
panic that saw the Dow Jones almost halve relative to its 1906 peak.
The stagflationary 1970s
The more recent example comes from the 1970s, another era of multiple supply
shocks. The biggest were the two oil shocks in 1973 and 1979, which both
represented negative supply shocks that pushed up inflation and hurt growth in the
US and Europe. However, this meant policymakers faced a dilemma. If central

banks cut rates, that would add to the inflationary pressures and risk worsening the
wage-price spirals already underway. Meanwhile, if fiscal stimulus were pursued,
that would also push up inflation.
Figure 8: US CPI (year-on-year % change)
-2
0
2
4
6
8

16
1955 1960 1965 1970 1975 1980 1985
First Oil Shock
Second Oil Shock
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
This context meant that policy couldn’t smooth out the business cycle as
successfully as it had in other decades. Indeed, in the decade from the first oil shock

in 1973, the US experienced 3 separate recessions. That is significantly more
turbulent than the last 40 years, when there have been just 4 recessions in total, or
once a decade on average. For financial markets too, the 1970s were a terrible
period for asset performance, with both equities and bonds losing ground in real
terms across multiple countries.
5. Implications
If economic policymakers can’t use traditional monetary and fiscal policy tools to

stabilise output and smooth the business cycle, then this will result in higher macro
volatility.
If we look back at the last 40 years, it is notable that the 5 most recent US expansions
all rank in the top 7 longest expansions since business-cycle dating began. This has
several causes, and some of it reflects a move away from an agriculture-based
economy where output was dependent on factors like the weather. But a key role
has also been played by pre-emptive policy interventions, such as cutting rates to

stop a slowdown turning into a recession. Indeed, recent examples include the a
dovish shifts in monetary policy after the 1987 stock market crash, the 1997-98

### Page 9

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
financial crises, and the mid-cycle slowdown of 2015-16.
Beyond the actual policy interventions, these moves also had a confidence effect
that supported financial markets. The fact that policymakers stood ready to take
action helped create a virtuous circle, as optimism on the economic outlook
boosted financial asset values, supported wealth effects, and avoided a tightening
in financial conditions that would itself cause growth to slow.

Source : NBER, Deutsche Bank
Clearly, policy is not as constrained today as in some periods of history. There is no

longer a gold standard where the convertibility needs to be maintained. And unlike
the 1970s, inflation is not in double-digits. Nevertheless, this is still a more
constrained world than we have been used to in the last 4 decades. Higher inflation
and more frequent supply shocks are constraining monetary policy. Meanwhile,
higher yields and higher public debt levels are constraining fiscal policy. We have
already seen the impacts since the pandemic, most notably in 2022, when central

banks hiked rates to combat the inflation surge, and there was a simultaneous
selloff across bonds and equities of a kind unseen in modern times.
Looking forward, this environment will offer new challenges of the sort we haven't
been used to in recent decades. The policy trade-offs are now more acute, and if
pre-emptive stabilisation is no longer available, then higher macro volatility lies
ahead.

Deutsche Bank Research Institute 
The end of the safety net: Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers – September 28, 2026  10

## Document 394: 「関西の景気動向」９月号 AI関連需要の拡大と関西の製造業生産（PDF：841KB）

Source PDF: all report pdf/「関西の景気動向」９月号　AI関連需要の拡大と関西の製造業生産（PDF：841KB）.pdf
Report source: JRI
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

2026 年９月 
株式会社 日本総合研究所 
調査部 関西経済研究センター 
http://www.jri.co.jp/report/medium/kansai/

１．景気の現状と展望 
 関西の景気は、緩やかな回復基調を維持しつつも、一部に弱い動きがみられる。企業部門では、円安傾向に
加えて、世界的なAI関連需要の拡大を背景に輸出額は増加している。AI関連投資の活発化などを背景に、
鉱工業生産にも持ち直しの動きがみられる。設備投資は、中東情勢の混乱などによる先行き不透明感が意識
されるなかでも、企業の収益計画が高めの水準を維持するもとで拡大基調が続いている。一方、家計部門では、
所得環境の改善が続くものの物価高が重石となり、個人消費の回復ペースは緩慢にとどまっている。 
先行きを展望すると、中東情勢の混乱が長引くなか、資源価格の高止まりが企業活動の重石となる。一方、
世界的な AI 関連需要の拡大が関西の生産や輸出に波及する動きが続き、設備投資の押し上げにもつながる
と見込まれる。個人消費は、持続的な賃上げの動きが下支えに寄与するものの、物価高による下押し圧力が
根強く、回復ペースは引き続き緩慢にとどまる見通しである。

２．個別指標の動き 
（１） 輸出・生産動向 
関西の輸出額（円ベース）は大きく増加している。国・地域別にみると、中国（香港を含む）向けや、アジ
ア（除く中国・香港）向けの伸びが大きく、ともに半導体等電子部品の増加がけん引している。米国向けは振
れを伴いつつも増加傾向にある。品目別にみると、電気機器では半導体等電子部品、一般機械では半導体
等製造装置の増加が顕著にみられ、世界的な AI 関連需要の高まりが押し上げに寄与しているとみられる。金
属及び同製品も先端材料としての需要増や、それに伴う市況の高騰を背景に高水準で推移している。 
一方、輸出を数量面でみると、回復傾向にあるものの、ペースは緩やかにとどまっている。中国向けやアジア
NIEs 向けは低水準での推移が続いているほか、ASEAN 向けも伸び悩んでいる。米国向けは振れを伴いつつ
も、基調としては底堅く推移している。日本銀行が公表している実質輸出指数にも増勢の加速はみられない。

2 
以上のように、輸出数量の回復が緩慢にとどまる一方、輸出金額が大幅に増加しており、両者の動きに乖離
がみられる。これは、円安による輸出金額の押し上げや、AI 関連需要の拡大を受けた関連財の価格高騰によ
る影響が大きい。

鉱工業生産は、持ち直しの動きが続いている。業種別にみると、半導体製造装置やデータセンター向け設備
など AI 関連需要の裾野の広がりが追い風となっている生産用機械工業や電気・情報通信機械工業、機能性
素材や医薬品の増産を受けた化学工業の上振れが目立つ。一方、電子部品・デバイス工業は依然として低
水準での推移が続いている（関西における電子部品・デバイス生産の低迷について、詳しくは後述のトピックス
を参照）。

（２） 企業収益・設備投資動向 
企業収益を日本銀行大阪支店「短観」（６月調査、全規模・全産業）でみると、関西企業全体の 2026
年度の経常利益（全産業・全規模）は前年度比▲12.9％と、減益見通しとなった。中東情勢の混乱が長
引くなか資源価格の高止まりなどが収益の下押し圧力となっている。もっとも、2025 年度が同＋16.3％と、価
格転嫁の浸透などを背景に想定を大きく上回って着地したため、その反動減という側面も大きい。コロナ禍前と
の比較では、引き続き高めの水準を維持している。

企業が高水準の収益を見込むもとで、関西企業は積極的な設備投資姿勢を維持している。日本銀行大
阪支店「短観」６月調査による2026年度の設備投資計画（全産業・全規模）は前年度比＋9.7％と、過
去（2010～2024 年度）の平均伸び率を上回る水準にある。機械設備や建設資材の価格上昇などから名
目の投資額が押し上げられている点は割り引く必要があるものの、中東情勢の緊迫化が企業の設備投資マイ
ンドに与える影響は今のところ限定的にとどまっている。ソフトウエア投資も前年度比＋5.1％と増勢が続いてお
り、効率化や省力化を目的とした企業の積極的な投資姿勢に変化はみられない。

（３） 雇用・所得動向 
関西の雇用環境は、人手不足を背景に良好な状態が続いている。雇用関連指標をみると、有効求人倍率
は１倍を超え、完全失業率（季節調整値）は 2％台後半の低位で推移している。雇用者数は高めの水準
を保っている。 
所得面をみると、府県別「毎月勤労統計調査」から推計した４～６月期の関西の一人当たり現金給与総
額は前年同期比＋4.3％と、前期に比べてプラス幅が拡大した。今年の春闘における高い賃上げ水準での妥
結や、夏季賞与の増加（大阪府内企業を対象とする調査では前年比＋2.5％）などを反映し、賃金は高め

4 
の伸びとなった。雇用者報酬を実質ベースでみると、物
価高による下押し圧力の緩和も相まって、4～６月期
は前年同期比＋３％超の増加となった。もっとも、春
先以降の原油高の影響がラグを伴って波及すると予想
されるほか、中東情勢の混乱が長期化するなか、原油
価格は依然として高水準にある。先行き、物価の伸び
は再び拡大する公算が大きく、実質所得には下押し圧
力が強い状況が続く見込みである。

（４） 消費動向 
関西の小売販売額をみると、スーパー、コンビニエンスストア、百貨店の販売額は緩やかに増加している。 
需要側統計（家計調査）をみると、実質ベースで見た消費支出は、4 月に税負担の軽減が実施された自
動車購入の増加などによる一時的な押し上げがみられたものの、均してみれば、物価高への警戒感が根強いも
とで、回復の勢いは鈍い。 
消費者マインドは、中東情勢の混乱直後の大幅
な悪化から改善傾向にあるものの、依然として悪化
前の水準を回復していない。とりわけ、大幅な落ち込
みがみられた「暮らし向き」や「耐久消費財の買い時
判断」の改善が遅れている。地政学リスクの長期化を
背景とした物価高への根強い警戒感が消費者マイン
ドの重石となっている。 
先行きの個人消費は、賃上げの持続が下支えに
寄与するものの、消費者物価の伸びが再び拡大する
もとで、回復ペースは緩慢にとどまる公算が大きい。

（５）住宅・公共投資動向 
関西の新設住宅着工戸数は、2025 年
４月の法改正を受けて上下に大きく振れた
ものの、 その後は落ち着きを取り戻し、概ね
法改正の影響を受ける前の水準に復してい
る。住宅需要は、金利の上昇や建築費の高
騰などによる下押し圧力が強いものの、将来
の資産価値への期待や、金利・建築費の先
高観を背景とした需要の高まりが下支えとな
り、大幅な落ち込みには至っていない。

2026 年７月の公共工事請負金額は前
年同月比＋15.0％となった。国土強靱化や
インフラ更新需要のほか、建設コストの上昇な
どを背景に、概ね前年を上回るペースで推移
している。

（６）倒産件数 
関西の企業倒産件数は、振れを伴いながらも、均してみるとコロナ禍前を上回る水準で推移している。原材
料費の高騰や人件費の上昇などに加え、金利上昇局面における資金繰りの悪化が懸念される。金利の上昇
によって新たに赤字に陥る中小企業の割合は全国よりも関西の方が大きいとの試算もあり、倒産件数は高止ま
りする可能性が高い。
